Left-Linear Completion with AC Axioms Thanks: The research described in this article is partly supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Number JP22K11900.
Johannes Niederhauser ORCID:0000-0002-8662-6834, Nao Hirokawa ORCID:0000-0002-8499-0501, Aart Middeldorp ORCID:0000-0001-7366-8464

Abstract
. We revisit completion modulo equational theories for left-linear term rewrite systems where unification modulo the theory is avoided and the normal rewrite relation can be used in order to decide validity questions. To that end, we give a new correctness proof for finite runs and establish a simulation result between the two inference systems known from the literature. Given a concrete reduction order, novel canonicity results show that the resulting complete systems are unique up to the representation of their rules’ right-hand sides. Furthermore, we show how left-linear AC completion can be simulated by general AC completion. In particular, this result allows us to switch from the former to the latter at any point during a completion process.

1.
Introduction

Completion has been extensively studied since its introduction in the
seminal paper by Knuth and Bendix
[
KB70
]
. One of the main
limitations of the original formulation is its inability to deal with
equations which cannot be oriented into a terminating rule such as the
commutativity axiom. This shortcoming can be resolved by completion
modulo an equational theory
\mathcal{E}
. In the literature, there are two
different approaches of achieving this. The general approach

[
JK86
,
Bac91
]
requires
\mathcal{E}
-unification and allows us to decide
validity problems using the rewrite relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{E}}
which is
defined as
\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
. For
left-linear term rewrite systems, however, there is Huet’s approach

[
Hue80
]
which avoids
\mathcal{E}
-unification. In particular, Huet’s
approach does not consider local peaks modulo
\mathcal{E}

(
\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
) but works with ordinary
local peaks (
\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
) as well as local cliffs of
the form
\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Hence, instead of

\mathcal{E}
-critical pairs we can use normal critical pairs when we also
take overlaps between
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{E}
into account. We call this
approach
left-linear completion modulo an equational theory
.
If we have a complete TRS
\mathcal{R}
in the general sense, we can decide
validity problems
s\approx t
by rewriting both terms to normal
form with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{E}}
and then checking the result for

\mathcal{E}
-equivalence. For complete systems stemming from left-linear

\mathcal{E}
-completion, however, it suffices to rewrite both
s
and
t

to normal form using the normal rewrite relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
and then
perform just one
\mathcal{E}
-equivalence check on these normal forms.

In their respective books, Avenhaus
[
Ave95
]
and Bachmair
[
Bac91
]

present inference systems for left-linear completion modulo an
equational theory. This article gives a detailed account of the
nature of and relation between these two systems for finite
runs. For the concrete case of AC (associative and commutative
function symbols), we compare left-linear completion modulo
equational theories with the general approach by presenting an
implementation of left-linear AC completion and comparing it with
the state of the art concerning general AC completion.
After setting the stage in Section
2
, we present a new
criterion for the Church–Rosser modulo property of left-linear TRSs
based on prime critical pairs in Section
3
. Slightly modified
versions (
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
) of the inference systems due to Avenhaus and
Bachmair are discussed in Sections
4
and

5
, respectively. Both sections include a new
correctness proof of the given inference system for finite runs. For

\mathsf{A}
, this is done from scratch by using the criterion from Section
3

in the spirit of
[
HMSW19
]
. Correctness of
\mathsf{B}
is then reduced to
the correctness of
\mathsf{A}
by establishing a simulation result between
finite runs in these systems. Furthermore, Section
6

reports on novel results on canonicity for this setting. For the
concrete equational theory of associative and commutative (AC)
function symbols, we also show the connection between the inference
system
\mathsf{A}
and general AC completion by means of another simulation
result (Section
7
). Finally, we describe our
implementation of
\mathsf{A}
in the tool
accompll
and present experimental
results which show that the avoidance of AC unification and AC
matching can result in significant performance improvements over
general AC completion (Sections
8
and

9
).

This article extends our previous papers
[
NHM23b
]
and

[
NHM23a
]
by including full proof details, more examples
and experimental data as well as novel results on canonicity.

2.
Preliminaries

We assume familiarity with term rewriting and completion as described
e.g. in
[
BN98
,
Ter03
]
but recall some central notions
in Section
2.1
. The concept of (prime) critical peaks which
leads to the definition of (prime) critical pairs is introduced in
Section
2.2
. Finally, Section
2.3
provides the
necessary background for rewriting modulo equational theories.

2.1.
Rewrite Systems

An
abstract rewrite system
or
abstract reduction system

(ARS)
\mathcal{A}=\langle A,{\rightarrow}\rangle
is a set
A
together with a
binary relation
\rightarrow
on
A
. If
a\rightarrow b
for no
b
, then
a
is
called a
normal form
of
\mathcal{A}
. Otherwise, we say that
a

is
reducible
. The set of normal forms is denoted by

\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}})
. Given an arbitrary binary relation
\rightarrow
, we write
\leftarrow
,

\leftrightarrow
,
\rightarrow^{=}
,
\rightarrow^{+}
and
\rightarrow^{*}
to denote its
inverse
, its

symmetric closure
, its
reflexive closure
, its

transitive closure
and its
symmetric and transitive closure
,
respectively. Hence, the relation
\leftrightarrow^{*}
denotes the
symmetric, reflexive and transitive closure of
\rightarrow
and is called

conversion
. The relation
a\rightarrow^{!}b
is defined as

a\rightarrow^{*}b
and
b\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}})
and is used to denote rewriting
to a normal form. Finally,
\downarrow
abbreviates
the
joinability relation

\rightarrow^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle*}$}}}$}}
where
\cdot

denotes the composition of binary relations
which is defined as follows:
R_{1}\cdot R_{2}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt(a,c)\mid\text{$(a,b)\in R_{1}$ and $(b,c)\in R_{2}$}\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.

Given a signature
\mathcal{F}
and a set of variables
\mathcal{V}
, we consider the
set of terms
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V})
which is defined as usual. The set of
variables in a term
t
is written as
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({t})
. Terms which do not
contain the same variable more than once are referred to as

linear
terms. Each subterm of a term
t
has a unique

position
which is a finite sequence of positive integers where
the empty sequence representing the root position is written as

\epsilon
. The set of positions in a term
t
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}({t})

and further divided into the subset
\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({t})
of positions
which address function symbols and the subset

\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{V}}({t})=\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}({t})\setminus\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({t})
of variable positions.
If a position
p
is a
prefix of the position
q
we write
p\leqslant q
. Positions
p
and
q

are parallel, denoted by
p\parallel q
, if neither
p\leqslant q

nor
q\leqslant p
. If
p\leqslant q
then
q\setminus p
denotes the unique
position
r
such that
pr=q
. By
s|_{p}
we denote the subterm
of
s
at position
p
. Mappings
\sigma
from variables to
terms with a finite domain (
\{\hskip 0.85358ptx\in\mathcal{V}\mid x\neq\sigma(x)\hskip 0.85358pt\}
) are
called
substitutions
. The application of a substitution
\sigma

to a term
t
is denoted by
t\sigma
. A
renaming
is a
bijective substitution from
\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{V}
. A term
s
is a

variant
of a term
t
(
s\doteq t
) if there exists a renaming

\sigma
such that
s=t\sigma
. A context
C
is a term with
exactly one occurrence of the special symbol
\Box\notin\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{V}

called
hole
which acts as a placeholder for concrete
terms. Replacing the hole with a term
t
results in a term which we
denote by
C[t]
. A term
s

encompasses
a term
t

(
s\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}t
) if
s=C[t\sigma]
for some context
C
and
substitution
\sigma
.
It is known that
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}
is a quasi-order on terms and its strict
part
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
is a well-founded order with

{\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}}={\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}}\setminus{\doteq}
. We write
s[t]_{p}
for the term
which is created from
s
by replacing its subterm at position
p
by
t
.

A pair of terms
(s,t)
can be viewed as an
equation
(
s\approx t
)
or a
rule
(
s\rightarrow t
). In the latter case we assume that
s
is not
a variable and
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({s})\supseteq\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({t})
.
Equational systems

(ESs) are sets of equations while
term rewrite systems
(TRSs)
are sets of rules. Given a set of pairs of terms
\mathcal{E}
, we define a

rewrite relation

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
as the closure of its pairs under
substitutions and contexts. More formally,
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}t
if there is
a pair
(\ell,r)\in\mathcal{E}
, a position
p
and a substitution
\sigma

such that
s|_{p}=\ell\sigma
and
t=s[r\sigma]_{p}
.
We sometimes make the position
p
explicit by writing

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{p}t
and define
\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{p}
as

\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathcal{E}}{\leftarrow}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{p}
. The

equational theory
induced by
\mathcal{E}
consists of all pairs of
terms
(s,t)
such that
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}t
. A TRS
\mathcal{R}

represents
an ES
\mathcal{E}
if
{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}}={\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}}
. Two
rules
\ell\to r
and

\ell^{\prime}\to r^{\prime}
are
variants
if there exists a renaming
\sigma

such that
\ell\sigma=\ell^{\prime}
and
r\sigma=r^{\prime}
. Two TRSs
\mathcal{R}_{1}

and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
over the same signature
\mathcal{F}
are
literally similar

(
\mathcal{R}_{1}\doteq\mathcal{R}_{2}
) if every rule in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
has a variant in
\mathcal{R}_{2}

and vice versa.
A TRS is

left-linear
if
\ell
is a linear term for every rule

{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\mathcal{R}
. Given an ES
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
denotes

\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\approx s\mid{s\approx t}\in\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.

A TRS
\mathcal{R}
is
terminating
if there is no infinite rewrite
sequence
s_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}s_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\cdots
. If

{\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}}
then
\mathcal{R}
is
confluent
. A TRS is

complete
if it is terminating and confluent. For standard
rewriting, the
Church–Rosser property

(
{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}}\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}
) coincides with confluence.
Hence, complete presentations
\mathcal{R}
of an ES
\mathcal{E}
can be used to
decide the validity problem for
\mathcal{E}
:
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}t
if and only
if
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{!}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}t
.

2.2.
Critical Peaks

Confluence of terminating TRSs is characterized by joinability of
critical pairs. Critical pairs are computed from overlaps which
are the potentially dangerous cases of nondeterminism in rewriting
as long as termination holds.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a TRS. An
overlap
is a triple

\langle{\ell_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}},p,{\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}}\rangle

satisfying the following properties:

\triangleright

\ell_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}
and
\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}
are variants of rewrite rules of

\mathcal{R}
without common variables,

\triangleright

p\in\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({\ell_{2}})
,

\triangleright

\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}|_{p}
are unifiable and

\triangleright

if
p=\epsilon
then
\ell_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}
and
\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}
are not
variants.

Overlaps give rise to
critical peaks
from which the critical
pairs can then be extracted.

{defi}

Let
\langle{\ell_{1}\rightarrow r_{1}},p,{\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}}\rangle
be an
overlap of a TRS
\mathcal{R}
and
\sigma
a most general unifier of

\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}|_{p}
. The term

\ell_{2}\sigma[\ell_{1}\sigma]_{p}=\ell_{2}\sigma
can be rewritten in
two different ways which gives rise to a critical peak, here
illustrated graphically:
\ell_{2}\sigma[\ell_{1}\sigma]_{p}=\ell_{2}\sigma
\ell_{2}\sigma[r_{1}\sigma]_{p}
r_{2}\sigma

p

r_{1}\leftarrow\ell_{1}

\epsilon

\ell_{2}\rightarrow r_{2}

More formally, a critical peak is a quadruple

\langle\ell_{2}\sigma[r_{1}\sigma]_{p},p,\ell_{2}\sigma,r_{2}\sigma\rangle

and the equation
\ell_{2}\sigma[r_{1}\sigma]_{p}\approx r_{2}\sigma
is a
critical pair of
\mathcal{R}
obtained from the original overlap.

Usually, we denote a critical peak
\langle t,p,s,u\rangle
more
illustratively by
t\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{p}s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{\epsilon}u
. The set of all
critical pairs of a TRS
\mathcal{R}
(obtained from all possible overlaps) is
denoted by
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})
.
Knuth and Bendix’ criterion
[
KB70
]
states that a terminating TRS

\mathcal{R}
is confluent if and only if
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}
.
Kapur et al.
[
KMN88
]
showed that joinability of prime
critical pairs still guarantees confluence for terminating TRSs.

{defi}

A critical peak
t\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{p}s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{\epsilon}u
is
prime

if all proper subterms of
s|_{p}
are in normal form. Critical pairs
derived from prime critical peaks are called prime. The set of all prime
critical pairs of a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is denoted by
\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})
.

{thmC}

[
[
KMN88
]
]

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a terminating TRS. The TRS
\mathcal{R}
is
confluent if and only if
\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}
. ∎

{exa}

Using Theorem
2.2
, we show confluence of the following TRS
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{b}

Since every rewrite step reduces the total number of
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{a}
,
termination of
\mathcal{R}
follows. Observe that
\mathcal{R}
admits six (modulo
symmetry) critical peaks of the form

t\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon}u
:

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{b}\vphantom{|}

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{b}\vphantom{|}

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{b})}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{b}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{b}\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{a}}+x)\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(x+\underline{\mathsf{a}})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

Here the positions
p
in
s
are indicated by underlining. The first
three critical peaks are not prime due to the reducible proper subterm

\mathsf{a}
in
s|_{p}
, while the others are prime. Therefore,

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{b}\approx\mathsf{b},\,\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Since
\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}

holds,
\mathcal{R}
is confluent.

2.3.
Rewriting Modulo

We now turn our attention to rewriting modulo an equational theory. To
that end, we start by giving general definitions for abstract rewrite
systems (ARSs). Let
\mathcal{A}=\langle A,{\rightarrow}\rangle
be an ARS and

\sim
an equivalence relation on
A
. We write

\Leftrightarrow
for
\leftarrow\cup\rightarrow\cup\sim
(conversion modulo
\sim
),

{\rightarrow}/{\sim}
for
\sim\cdot\rightarrow\cdot\sim
(rewriting modulo
\sim
)
and
\downarrow^{\sim}
for
\rightarrow^{*}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle*}$}}}$}}

(valley modulo
\sim
). In order to simplify our terminology,
we sometimes refer to conversions modulo
\sim
as conversions. Hence,
whether conversions include
\sim
-steps or not depends on the type of
rewriting we consider. Given
\mathcal{A}
, we
denote
\langle A,{{\rightarrow}/{\sim}}\rangle
by
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
. The ARS

\mathcal{A}
is
terminating modulo
\sim
if there are no infinite
rewrite sequences with
{\rightarrow}/{\sim}
and

Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
if

{\Leftrightarrow^{*}}\subseteq{\downarrow^{\sim}}
.

Confluence and the
Church–Rosser property do not coincide for rewriting modulo
equational theories
[
Ohl98
]
. Therefore, a notion of completeness
for this setting has to depend on the Church–Rosser property instead
of confluence in order to facilitate a decision procedure for validity
problems of equational theories with complete presentations.
Hence, we define an ARS
\mathcal{A}
to be
complete modulo
\sim

if it is terminating modulo

\sim
and Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
. While there is no distinction
for termination modulo
\sim
between
\mathcal{A}
and
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}

(
{\sim\cdot\sim}={\sim}
by transitivity), it makes a
considerable difference whether we talk about the Church–Rosser
modulo
\sim
property and therefore completeness modulo
\sim
of

\mathcal{A}
or
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
.

{exa}

Consider the ARS
\mathcal{A}
together with the equivalence relation
\sim

defined as follows:

\mathsf{a}\sim\mathsf{b}\rightarrow\mathsf{c}

Since
\mathsf{a}\Leftrightarrow^{*}\mathsf{c}
and

\mathsf{a}\mathrel{{\rightarrow}/{\sim}}\mathsf{c}
, the ARS
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
. However,
\mathcal{A}
is not
Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
as
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{c}

are different normal forms of
\rightarrow
which are not
equivalent in
\sim
.

The following lemma is taken from

[
Ave95
, Lemma 4.1.12]
. It establishes an important connection
between the Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
property of an ARS
\mathcal{A}
and

{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
.

{lemC}

[
[
Ave95
]
]

Let
\mathcal{A}=\langle A,{\rightarrow}\rangle
and

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\langle A,{\rightharpoonup}\rangle
be ARSs and

\sim
an equivalence relation on
A
such that

{\rightarrow}\subseteq{\rightharpoonup}\subseteq{{\rightarrow}/{\sim}}
. If

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
then
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
. ∎
Note that the implication cannot be strengthened to an equivalence due
to Example
2.3
. The following example illustrates a successful
use of Lemma
2.3
.

{exa}

Consider the ARS
\mathcal{A}=\langle A,{\rightarrow}\rangle
from Example
2.3
.
For
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\langle A,{\rightharpoonup}\rangle
with

{\rightharpoonup}={\sim\cdot\rightarrow}
we can easily establish
the Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
property. Hence, by
Lemma
2.3
also
{\mathcal{A}}/{\sim}
is Church–Rosser
modulo
\sim
.

The definitions and results for ARSs carry over to TRSs by replacing
the equivalence relation
\sim
by the equational theory

\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
of an ES
\mathcal{B}
. Most theoretical results of this
article are not specific to AC but hold for an arbitrary base theory

\mathcal{B}
of which we only demand that
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({\ell})=\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({r})
for all

{\ell\approx r}\in\mathcal{B}
. We abbreviate
\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
by
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}

and the rewrite relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
is defined as

\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
. Furthermore, we write

\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}
for the relation

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}
. Note
that the omission of
\mathcal{B}
in the notation
\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}
poses no
problem as
\mathcal{B}
is usually fixed and can be inferred from
the context in all other cases.
Termination modulo

\mathcal{B}
is shown by
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction orders

>
, i.e.,

>
is well-founded, closed under contexts and substitutions and

{\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot>\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}}\subseteq{>}
. Note that
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}

is not a very practical rewrite relation: It is undecidable in
general, and even if the equational theory of
\mathcal{B}
is decidable,
rewriting a term
t
requires to check every member of its

\mathcal{B}
-equivalence class. A more practical alternative due to Peterson
and Stickel
[
PS81
]
is the relation

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}}
defined as follows:

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}}t
if there exist a rule
\ell\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
,
a substitution
\sigma
and a position
p
such that

s|_{p}\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\ell\sigma
and
t=s[r\sigma]_{p}
. This definition is
very similar to the definition of standard rewriting but with

\mathcal{B}
-matching instead of normal matching. It is immediate from the
respective definitions that the inclusions

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}}
hold.

{exa}

Consider the TRS

\mathcal{R}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+y

\displaystyle\rightarrow y

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)+y

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{s}(x+y)

where
+
is an AC symbol. We have
y+\mathsf{s}(x)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\mathsf{s}(x+y)
as

y+\mathsf{s}(x)\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\mathsf{s}(x)+y
but
y+\mathsf{s}(x)
is a normal form with
respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Furthermore,
(y+z)+\mathsf{s}(x)\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}\mathsf{s}(x+y)+z

since
(y+z)+\mathsf{s}(x)\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}(\mathsf{s}(x)+y)+z
but this step is not
possible with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}
as the rewrite step takes place at position
1

whereas we need to search for AC equivalent terms at the root position.

3.
Church–Rosser Criterion

In this section we present a new characterization of the Church–Rosser
property modulo an equational theory
\mathcal{B}
for left-linear TRSs which are
terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}
. The original left-linear completion
procedure
[
Ave95
,
Bac91
]
relies on the following
theorem. We use critical pairs between two different TRSs
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and

\mathcal{R}_{2}
with the same signature. We write
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}})
for the set
of all critical pairs originating from overlaps

\langle\rho_{1},p,\rho_{2}\rangle
where
\rho_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
and

\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
. The union of
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}})
and

\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{1}})
is denoted by
\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}})
.

{thmC}

[
[
Hue80
]
]

A left-linear TRS
\mathcal{R}
which is terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}

is Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
if and only if

\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
. ∎

{exa}

[continued from Example
2.2
]

Let
\mathcal{B}=\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+y\approx y+x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. We show that the left-linear
TRS
\mathcal{R}
of Example
2.2
is Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
. As before, the
termination of
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}
follows from the fact that every rewrite step
reduces the total number of
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{a}
. The six critical peaks
result in
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{a}\approx\mathsf{a},\,\mathsf{a}\approx\mathsf{b},\,\mathsf{b}\approx\mathsf{a},\,\mathsf{b}\approx\mathsf{b},\,\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.
Observe that
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{B}
admit four critical peaks of the forms

t\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{\epsilon}u
or

t\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon}u
:

\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{a}+x})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{a})\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{x+\mathsf{a}})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+x)\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{b}+x})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{x+\mathsf{b}})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\vphantom{|}
x\vphantom{|}

Thus,
\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{a})\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+x)\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. It is easy to see that
t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
for all critical
pairs
t\approx u
in
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
.
Hence, by Theorem
3
, the TRS
\mathcal{R}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
.

Theorem
3
allows us to use ordinary critical pairs
instead of
\mathcal{B}
-critical pairs, i.e., critical pairs stemming from
local peaks modulo
\mathcal{B}
(
\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
)
[
JK86
]
.
In particular, equational unification
modulo
\mathcal{B}
can be replaced by syntactic unification which improves
efficiency. Furthermore, the form of the joining sequence
(
\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}
) is advantageous as it uses the normal rewrite
relation and just one
\mathcal{B}
-equality check in the end as opposed to
rewrite steps modulo the theory (
\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
).
However, left-linearity is necessary in Theorem
3

as the following example illustrates.

{exa}

Consider the AC-terminating TRS
\mathcal{R}
consisting of the single rule

\mathsf{f}(x,x)\rightarrow x
with
+
as an additional AC function symbol as well as
the following conversion:

x+y\,\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\,\mathsf{f}(x+y,x+y)\,\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\,\mathsf{f}(x+y,y+x)

There are no critical pairs in
\mathcal{R}
and between
\mathcal{R}
and

\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
, so
\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})=\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}})=\varnothing
.
However,
x+y\,\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}\,\mathsf{f}(x+y,y+x)

does not hold because
x+y
and
\mathsf{f}(x+y,y+x)
are
\mathcal{R}
-normal
forms which are not AC equivalent. Thus,
\mathcal{R}
is not
Church–Rosser modulo AC.

Even though most results in this article only apply to
left-linear TRSs, we do not demand that
\mathcal{B}
is linear (i.e.,
having equations only consisting of linear terms). The reason
for that can be found in the
proof of Theorem
3
where left-linearity is crucial but only needed for
rewrite rules: If
\mathcal{R}
is not left-linear, the proof reveals that
we also have to consider variable overlaps (i.e., overlaps at variable
positions) between
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{B}
. This would make a confluence analysis
solely based on critical pairs impossible.
In the remainder of this section we show that joinability of
prime critical pairs suffices for the characterization of
Theorem
3
.

3.1.
Peak-and-Cliff Decreasingness

We present a new Church–Rosser modulo criterion, dubbed

peak-and-cliff decreasingness
. This is an extension of
peak decreasingness
[
HMSW19
]
which is a simple confluence criterion
for ARSs designed to replace complicated proof orderings in the
correctness proofs of completion procedures. As such,
peak-and-cliff decreasingness will be a crucial ingredient
in the correctness proof we give for Avenhaus’ inference system
in Section
4
.

In the following, we assume that equivalence relations
\sim
are
defined as the reflexive and transitive closure of a symmetric
relation

an equivalence relation on
A
. Note that several different steps
can have the same label. Furthermore, for the sake of better readability,
we allow ourselves some freedom of where to annotate our arrow relations
with labels, closure operators (reflexive, transitive, …) and the like.
This will cause no confusion.

{defi}

An ARS
\mathcal{A}
is
peak-and-cliff decreasing
if there
is a well-founded order
>
on
I
such that for all
\alpha,\beta\in I

the inclusions

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\alpha}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{\xLeftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.85358pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\vee\alpha\beta\>$}}]{*}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\alpha}$}}}$}}\cdot\mathrel{\mathchoice{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.42 0 L 12.04 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.29999pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.42 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.70003pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.04 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\kern 0.20004pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{\xLeftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.85358pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\vee\alpha\>$}}]{*}\cdot\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\beta\>$}}]{=}}

hold. Here
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\displaystyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\textstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle<$}}}\alpha\beta
denotes the set

\{\hskip 0.85358pt\gamma\in I\mid\text{$\alpha>\gamma$ or $\beta>\gamma$}\hskip 0.85358pt\}

and if
J\subseteq I
then
\rightarrow_{J}
denotes

\bigcup\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt{\rightarrow_{\gamma}}\mid\gamma\in J\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. We abbreviate

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\displaystyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\textstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle<$}}}\alpha\alpha
to
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\displaystyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\textstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptstyle<$}}}{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{\rotatebox{90.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle<$}}}\alpha
.

{exa}

Let
I=\{\hskip 0.85358pt1,2\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and
consider the following
I
-labeled ARS

\mathcal{A}=\langle A,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\rightarrow_{1},\rightarrow_{2}\hskip 0.85358pt\}\rangle

equipped with the equivalence relation

{\sim}=({\mathrel{\mathchoice{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

and

\mathsf{d}\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle 1}$}}}$}}\mathsf{b}
, respectively.

We show that peak-and-cliff decreasingness is a sufficient condition
for the Church–Rosser modulo property.

Proof 3.1
.

We abbreviate

to
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}}
.
Suppose
a\Leftrightarrow^{n}b
. We show
a\downarrow^{\sim}b
or
a\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}}b
by
induction on
n
. If
n=0
then
a=b
and therefore also

a\downarrow^{\sim}b
. If
n>0
then

a\Leftrightarrow c\Leftrightarrow^{n-1}b
for some
c
. The induction hypothesis
yields
c\downarrow^{\sim}b
or
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}}b
. In the latter case we are
already done because
{\Leftrightarrow\cdot\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\displaystyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\textstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\leftharpoonup$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$\cr}}}}}
. In
the former case, note that there exists a
k
such that

c\rightarrow^{k}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle*}$}}}$}}b
. We continue with a
case analysis on
k
:

\triangleright

k=0
:
From
a\Leftrightarrow c
we obtain
a\rightarrow c
,
a\leftarrow c
or
a\sim c
.
If
a\rightarrow c
we immediately obtain
a\downarrow^{\sim}b
.
If
a\leftarrow c
we have
a\leftarrow c\sim c^{\prime}\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle*}$}}}$}}b
for some
c^{\prime}
. Now

c=c^{\prime}
and hence
a\downarrow^{\sim}b

or

\triangleright

.

The proof of the following theorem relies on the fact that the
well-founded order on an index set obtained from peak-and-cliff
decreasingness
can be extended to a well-founded order on multisets of labels.
Here, the multiset extension of an order
>
is defined as follows:

M_{1}>_{\mathsf{mul}}M_{2}
if
M_{2}=(M_{1}\setminus X)\uplus Y
where

\varnothing\neq X\subseteq M_{1}
and for all
y\in Y
there
exists an
x\in X
such that
x>y
. It is well-known that the multiset
extension of a well-founded order is also well-founded
[
DM79
]
.

Theorem 2
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a peak-and-cliff decreasing
ARS
then
\mathcal{A}

is Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
.

Proof 3.2
.

With every conversion
C
we associate a multiset
M_{C}
consisting
of labels of its rewrite and equivalence relation steps. Since
\mathcal{A}

is peak-and-cliff decreasing, there is a well-founded order
>
on

I
which allows us to replace conversions
C
of the forms

\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\alpha}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\beta}
,

is Church–Rosser

\sim

{\Leftrightarrow^{*}}\subseteq{\downarrow^{\sim}}

>_{\mathsf{mul}}

C

1
we

C
is
empty) or one of the following cases holds:

If
a\downarrow^{\sim}b
we are immediately done. In the remaining cases,
we have a local peak or cliff with concrete labels
\alpha
and

\beta
, so
M_{C}=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pt\alpha,\beta\hskip 0.85358pt\}\uplus\Gamma_{2}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is peak-and-cliff decreasing, there is a conversion
C^{\prime}
with

M_{C^{\prime}}=\Gamma_{1}\uplus\Gamma\uplus\Gamma_{3}
where

\{\hskip 0.85358pt\alpha,\beta\hskip 0.85358pt\}>_{\mathsf{mul}}\Gamma
. Hence,
M_{C}>_{\mathsf{mul}}M_{C^{\prime}}
and
we finish the proof by applying the induction hypothesis.

The above theorem can also be shown by verifying that it is a special
case of the Church–Rosser modulo criterion known as decreasing
diagrams
[
FvO13
, Theorem 31]
. Note, however, that it is not as
obvious as the fact that peak decreasingness
[
HMSW19
]
is an instance
of decreasing diagrams for confluence
[
vO94
]
.

1

Pointed out
by Vincent van Oostrom (personal communication).

For the main result of this section, a simpler version of
peak-and-cliff decreasingness suffices. The full power
of peak-and-cliff decreasingness will be needed in the
correctness proof of Avenhaus’ inference system in
Section
4
.

a\rightarrow c

(a\mathrel{\mathchoice{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.42 0 L 12.04 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.29999pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.42 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.70003pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.04 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\kern 0.20004pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.42 0 L 12.04 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.29999pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.42 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.70003pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.04 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\kern 0.20004pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.42 0 L 12.04 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.29999pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.42 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.70003pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.04 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\kern 0.20004pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 0.50003pt\hbox to9.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.42 0 L 12.04 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.29999pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.42 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.70003pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 12.04 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=rect} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.14 -3.6 L 0.14 3.6}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\kern 0.20004pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}c)
. Furthermore,
\xLeftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.85358pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\vee a\>$}}]{*}

denotes the binary relation consisting of all pairs of elements
which are connected by a conversion where each step is labeled by an
element smaller than
a
.

Corollary 3
.

Every
ARS
that is source decreasing modulo
\sim
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
.

Proof 3.3
.

In the definition of peak-and-cliff decreasingness we set
I=A
. Note that
this implies
\alpha=\beta
for all local peaks and cliffs. Hence,
the ARS is peak-and-cliff decreasing and we can conclude by Theorem
2
.

3.2.
Prime Critical Pairs

We show that joinability of prime critical pairs is enough for
characterizing the Church–Rosser modulo property.
In the following,
\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
denotes the restriction of

\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
to prime critical pairs but where
irreducibility is always checked with respect to
\mathcal{R}
, i.e., the
critical peaks
t\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{\epsilon}u
and

t^{\prime}\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{p}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\epsilon}u^{\prime}
are both prime if
proper subterms of
s|_{p}
are irreducible with respect to
\mathcal{R}
.

{exa}

[continued from Example
3
]

Recall the four critical peaks between
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{B}
. The first two
peaks

\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{a})\mathrel{\prescript{1}{\mathcal{B}}{\leftarrow}}\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{a}+x})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}x

and

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}+x)\mathrel{\prescript{1}{\mathcal{B}}{\leftarrow}}\mathsf{f}(\underline{x+\mathsf{a}})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}x

are not prime due to the reducible proper subterm
\mathsf{a}
. The other two
are prime. Hence,
\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}+x)\approx x,\,\mathsf{f}(x+\mathsf{b})\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.

Correctness of Theorem
3
can be shown by the combination of
with the following lemma.

{lemC}

[
[
Hue80
]
]

For left-linear TRSs
\mathcal{R}
, the following inclusion holds:

{\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}}\,\subseteq\,{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}\cup\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\,\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}}

∎

In order to integrate the refinement by prime critical pairs some more
observations are required.
Note that for the original refinement by Kapur et al.
[
KMN88
]
,
correctness is shown in the context of general AC rewriting by flattening
terms with AC symbols. We employ our novel notion of peak-and-cliff
decreasingness instead. Our proof can be seen as an extension of the
corresponding proof given for the ordinary Church–Rosser property in

[
HMSW19
]
.

{defi}

Given a TRS
\mathcal{R}
and terms
s
,
t
and
u
, we write

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u
if
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}t
,
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}u
and

t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
or
t\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})}u
. We write
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
if

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}t
,
s\sim u
and
t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
or

t\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}u
. Furthermore,

{\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\triangledown_{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle s$}}^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\sim}$}}}$}}}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt(u,t)\mid t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.

Note that the joinability of ordinary critical peaks is not affected
by incorporating
\mathcal{B}
into conversions. Hence, the following result
is taken from
[
HMSW19
, Lemma 2.15]
and therefore stated without
proof. Here,
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{2}}u
means that there is a term
v

with
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}v
and
v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u
.

{lemC}

[
[
HMSW19
]
]

If
t\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{p}s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\epsilon}u
is
a critical peak of a TRS
\mathcal{R}
then
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{2}}u
. ∎

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a left-linear
TRS
.

(1)

If
t\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{p}s\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{\epsilon}u
is a critical peak then

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
.

(2)

If
t\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{p}s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\epsilon}u
is a critical peak then

t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\triangledown_{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle s$}}^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\sim}$}}}$}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u
.

Proof 3.4
.

We only prove (1) as the other statement is symmetrical. If all
proper subterms of
s|_{p}
are in normal form with respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
,

t\approx u\in\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
which establishes

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
. Since also
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}t
, we obtain the desired
result. Otherwise, there are a position
q>p
and a term
v
such
that
s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{q}v
and all proper subterms of
s|_{q}
are in
normal form with respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Together with
Lemma
3.2
we obtain
v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
or

v\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}u
. In both cases
v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u

holds. A similar case analysis applies to the local peak

t\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{p}s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{q}v
: By the Critical Pair Lemma, either

t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}v
or
t\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R}})}v
. In the latter case

v|_{p}\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{q\setminus p}s|_{p}\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\epsilon}t|_{p}

is an instance of a prime critical peak as
q>p
and all proper
subterms of
s|_{q}
are in normal form with respect to

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Closure of rewriting under contexts and substitutions
yields
t\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})}v
. Therefore, we have
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}v
in
both cases, concluding the proof.

The following lemma generalizes the previous results of this section
to arbitrary local peaks and cliffs.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a left-linear
TRS
.

(1)

If
t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
then
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{2}}u
.

(2)

If
t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}u
then
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
.

Proof 3.5
.

We only prove (2) as the proof of (1) (which depends on the Critical
Pair Lemma) can be found in
[
HMSW19
, Lemma 2.16]
. Let

t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}u
. From Lemma
3.2
we obtain

t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
or
t\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\,\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}u
. In the
former case we are done as
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u
. For the
latter case we further distinguish between the two subcases

t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R},\,\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}u
and
u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{B}^{\pm},\,\mathcal{R}})}t
.
Note that this list of subcases is exhaustive due to the
direction of the local cliff. If
t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{R},\,\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}u
,

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
follows from Lemma
4
(1) and
closure of rewriting under contexts and substitutions. If

u\rightarrow_{\mathsf{CP}({\mathcal{B}^{\pm},\,\mathcal{R}})}t
,
u\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\triangledown_{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle s$}}^{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\sim}$}}}$}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}t
and
therefore
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}\cdot\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
follows from
Lemma
4
(2) as well as closure of rewriting under contexts
and substitutions.

Now, we are able to prove the main result of this section, a novel
necessary and sufficient condition for the Church–Rosser property
modulo an equational theory
\mathcal{B}
which strengthens the original
result from
[
Hue80
]
to prime critical pairs.

Theorem 6
.

A left-linear
TRS

\mathcal{R}
which is terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}

is Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
if and only if

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
.

Proof 3.6
.

The only-if direction is trivial. For a proof of the if
direction, we show that
\mathcal{R}
is source decreasing modulo
\mathcal{B}
; the
Church–Rosser property modulo
\mathcal{B}
is then an immediate
consequence of . From the termination of
\mathcal{R}
modulo

\mathcal{B}
we obtain the well-founded order
{>}={\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}
.

Consider an arbitrary local peak
t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
.
Lemma
5
(1) yields a term
v
such that

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}u
. Together with

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
we obtain

t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
. By definition,

s>t,v,u
so the corresponding condition required by source
decreasingness modulo
\mathcal{B}
is fulfilled.

Now consider an arbitrary local cliff
t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}u
.
Lemma
5
(2) yields a term
v
such that

t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
. Together with

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
we
obtain
t\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}u
. By definition,

s>t,v
and
s\sim u
. The conversion between
v
and
u
is of
the form
v\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{k}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}u
for some

k
. If
k=0
then all steps between
v
and
u
can be labeled with
terms which are smaller than
s
. If
k>0
then there exists a

w<s
such that

v\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{k-1}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}w\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}u
. In
this case all steps of the conversion are labeled with terms which
are smaller than
s
except for the rightmost step which we may
label with
s
. Hence, the corresponding condition required by
source decreasingness modulo
\mathcal{B}
is fulfilled in all cases.

{exa}

[continued from Example
3.2
]
One can verify the termination of
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}
and the inclusion

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
.
By Theorem
6
the Church–Rosser modulo property holds.

Finally, we show that the previous result does not hold if we just
demand termination of
\mathcal{R}
. The counterexample shows this for the
concrete case of AC and is based on Example 4.1.8 from
[
Ave95
]

which uses an ARS. Note that the usage of prime critical pairs instead
of critical pairs has no effect.

{exa}

Consider the TRS
\mathcal{R}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})+\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})+\mathsf{b}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{d}

where
+
is an AC function symbol. Clearly, the (prime) critical
pairs of
\mathcal{R}
are joinable modulo AC because

\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})
. For

\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}})
we only have to consider the rules which
rewrite to
\mathsf{c}
and
\mathsf{d}
respectively since all other rules
only involve AC equivalent terms. Modulo symmetry, these (prime)
critical pairs are:

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})+\mathsf{b}

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}+x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{b}+((\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x+\mathsf{b})+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})+\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}+x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{a}+((\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x+\mathsf{a})+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})

Removing the joinable (prime) critical pairs leaves us with

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}+x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{b}+((\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x+\mathsf{b})+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}+x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{a}+((\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{d}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x+\mathsf{a})+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})

which are not joinable at the moment. However, we can extend
\mathcal{R}

by the rewrite rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}+((\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a}))+x

\displaystyle(x+\mathsf{b})+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x+(\mathsf{b}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{a}))

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}+((\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})+x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a}))+x

\displaystyle(x+\mathsf{a})+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x+(\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a}))

in order to make them joinable. Note that the additional (prime)
critical pairs in
\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}})
caused by
adding the new rules are trivially joinable modulo AC as all of
these new critical pairs are AC equivalent. To sum up,

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})\cup\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}})\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}}
.
Termination of
\mathcal{R}
can be checked by
e.g. the termination tool

T
T
T
2

[
KSZM09
]
, but the loop

\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})\,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{a})\,\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\,\mathsf{a}+(\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{b})

shows that
\mathcal{R}
is not AC terminating. We have

\mathsf{c}\Leftrightarrow^{*}\mathsf{d}
but not
\mathsf{c}\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}\mathsf{d}
as
the terms are normal forms and not AC equivalent. Hence,
\mathcal{R}
is
not Church–Rosser modulo AC.

4.
Avenhaus’ Inference System

The idea of completion modulo an equational theory
\mathcal{B}
for
left-linear systems where the normal rewrite relation can be used to
decide validity problems has been put forward by Huet
[
Hue80
]
. To
the best of our knowledge, inference systems for this approach are
only presented in the books by Avenhaus
[
Ave95
]
and Bachmair

[
Bac91
]
. This section presents a new correctness proof of a version
of Avenhaus’ inference system for finite runs in the spirit of

[
HMSW19
]
which does not rely on proof orderings. Correctness of
Bachmair’s system is established by a simulation result in
Section
5
.

4.1.
Inference System

{defi}

The inference system
\mathsf{A}
is parameterized by a fixed

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order
>
. It transforms pairs
consisting of an ES
\mathcal{E}
and a TRS
\mathcal{R}
over the common signature

\mathcal{F}
according to the following inference rules where

s\approx^{\pm}t
denotes either

s\approx t
or
t\approx s
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{\qquad}r@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{}}\textsf{deduce}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}&\text{if $s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t$}&\textsf{orient}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx^{\pm}t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}}&\text{if $s>t$}\\[17.07182pt]
\textsf{deduce}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\rightarrow s\hskip 0.85358pt\}}&\text{if $s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}t$}&\textsf{delete}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}}&\text{if $s\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t$}\\[17.07182pt]
\textsf{simplify}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx^{\pm}t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptu\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}&\text{if $s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}u$}&\textsf{collapse}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\to t\hskip 0.85358pt\}}{\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptu\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}&\text{if $s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u$}\\[17.07182pt]
&&&\textsf{compose}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow u\hskip 0.85358pt\}}&\text{if $t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}u$}\end{array}

A step in an inference system
I
from an ES
\mathcal{E}
and a TRS
\mathcal{R}

to an ES
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
and a TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is denoted by

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize{I} }}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
. The parentheses of the
pairs are only used when the expression is surrounded by text in order to
increase readability.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an ES. A finite sequence

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\cdots\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}

with
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\varnothing
is a
run
for

\mathcal{E}
. If
\mathcal{E}_{n}\neq\varnothing
, the run
fails
. The run is

fair
if
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is left-linear and the following inclusions hold:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}_{n}})

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}}\cup{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cup\mathcal{R}_{i}}}}}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}}\cup{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}}}}

Intuitively, fair and non-failing runs yield a
\mathcal{B}
-complete
presentation
\mathcal{R}_{n}
of the initial set of equations
\mathcal{E}
, i.e.,

{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}}^{*}}={\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\subseteq{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}}
. In particular, the inference rules are designed
to preserve the equational theory augmented by
\mathcal{B}
.

{exa}

In this example we illustrate a successful run for the ES
\mathcal{E}

consisting of the equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 1$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 2$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 3$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x

where
+
is an AC function symbol. This example is taken from

[
Ave95
, Example 4.2.15(b)]
. As suggested by Definition
4.1
we
only consider prime critical pairs. As AC-compatible reduction
order we use the polynomial interpretation
[
BL87
]

\displaystyle+_{\mathbb{N}}(x,y)

\displaystyle\,=\,x+y+1

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}_{\mathbb{N}}(x)

\displaystyle\,=\,x^{2}+x

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}_{\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\,=\,1

and start by orienting equations
2 and 3
into the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 2^{\prime}$}\,}\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 3^{\prime}$}\,}\,x

which only leads to prime critical pairs between rule 3
′

and
\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
. We add these prime critical pairs as rules by
applying
deduce
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+x

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 4$}\,}\,x

\displaystyle x+(y+\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 6$}\,}\,x+y

\displaystyle x+(\mathsf{0}+y)

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 5$}\,}}}\,x+y

\displaystyle(x+y)+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 7$}\,}}}\,x+y

Keeping rule 4
enables us to collapse the remaining three rules to the equations

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 5^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 6^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 7^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

which can all be deleted. Next, we deduce the prime critical pair
stemming from rules 3
′

and 4
which is just the
trivial equation
\mathsf{0}\approx\mathsf{0}
and therefore can be
deleted. We continue by deducing the prime critical pairs between
rule 4
and
\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
which adds the new rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 8$}\,}\,x+y

\displaystyle(\mathsf{0}+x)+y

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 9$}\,}\,x+y

\displaystyle(x+\mathsf{0})+y

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 10$}\,}\,x+y

which can all be collapsed to trivial equations

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 8^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 9^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 10^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,x+y

and therefore deleted. Now we orient the only remaining
original equation 1 to

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 1^{\prime}$}\,}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

which gives rise to two prime critical pairs between rules 1
′
and 3
′

as well as rule 4

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 11$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})+\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 12$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)

which can be simplified to the trivial equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 11^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 12^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)

and therefore deleted. Finally, we deduce rules corresponding to the
prime critical pairs between rule 1
′

and
\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(y+x)

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 13$}\,}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+(y+z))

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 14$}\,}\,\mathsf{f}(x+y)+\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}((x+y)+z)

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 15$}\,}}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y+z)

Applications of
collapse
and
simplify
transform these rules to AC
equivalent equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 13^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)+(\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(z))

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 14^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}\,(\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y))+\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y))+\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 15^{\prime}$}\,}}{{\approx}}}}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+(\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(z))

which can be deleted. Thus, the TRS consisting of the rules
1
′
, 2
′
, 3
′
and 4
is the result of a fair and non-failing run which is an AC complete
presentation of the original equations as we will show in the
correctness proof.

The next example shows that deducing local cliffs as rules instead
of equations as well as the restriction to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
in the
collapse
rule are
crucial properties of the inference system.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
consisting of the single equation

x+\mathsf{0}\,\approx\,x

where
+
is an AC function symbol. We clearly have

\mathsf{0}+x\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}x
, so an AC complete system
\mathcal{C}

representing
\mathcal{E}
has to satisfy
\mathsf{0}+x\downarrow_{\mathcal{C}}^{\sim}x
. There is just one way to orient the only equation in
\mathcal{E}
which
results in the rule
x+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x
. Since we want our run to be
fair, we add the rules stemming from the prime critical pairs
between
x+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x
and
\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+x

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle x+(\mathsf{0}+y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x+y

\displaystyle x+(y+\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x+y

\displaystyle(x+y)+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x+y

If collapsing with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
is allowed, all these rules become
trivial equations and can therefore be deleted. Thus, the modified
inference system allows for a fair run which is not complete as

\mathsf{0}+x\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}x
does not hold for

\mathcal{R}=\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Furthermore, if we add pairs of terms
stemming from local cliffs as equations, we get the same result by
applications of
simplify
.

The inference system presented in Definition
4.1
is almost the same
as the one presented by Avenhaus in
[
Ave95
]
. However, since we only
consider finite runs, the encompassment condition for the
collapse
rule
has been removed in the spirit of
[
ST13
]
. (The original side
condition is
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
with
\ell\rightarrow r\in\mathcal{R}
and
s\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}l
.)
The following example shows that this can lead to smaller

\mathcal{B}
-complete systems.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
consisting of the single equation

\mathsf{f}(x+y)\,\approx\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

where
+
is an AC function symbol. The inference system presented
in
[
Ave95
]
produces the AC complete system

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(y+x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

in which either of the rules could be collapsed if it was allowed to
collapse with the other rule. In
[
Ave95
]
this is prevented by an
encompassment condition which essentially forbids to collapse at the
root position with a rewrite rule whose left-hand side is a variant
of the left-hand side of the rule which should be
collapsed. However, this is possible with the system presented in
this article, so for an AC complete representation just one of the
two rules suffices.

4.2.
Correctness Proof

We show that every fair and non-failing finite run results in a

\mathcal{B}
-complete presentation. To this end, we first verify that
inference steps in
\mathsf{A}
preserve convertibility. We abbreviate

\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{B}
to

\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7
.

If
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
then the following inclusions hold:

(1)

{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\>$}}]{}}\,\subseteq\,{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{=}\cdot\mathrel{(\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{=}\cup\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{*})}\cdot\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{=}}

(2)

{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}}\,\subseteq\,{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{*}}

Proof 4.1
.

By inspecting the inference rules of
\mathsf{A}
we obtain the following
inclusions:

deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup{\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}}

\displaystyle\cup{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}}\cup{\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{}}

orient

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}\cup(\mathcal{E})^{-1}\cup\mathcal{R}

delete

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup{\sim_{\mathcal{B}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

compose

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{}}

collapse

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup{\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{}}

simplify

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{}}$}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
