\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{R}\cup{\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}}/\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\>$}}]{}}\cup{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\>$}}]{}\cdot\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}}/\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{}}$}

Then, inclusion (2) follows directly from the closure of rewrite
relations under contexts and substitutions. Statement (1) holds
since it is a generalization that all cases have in common.

Corollary 8
.

If
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
then

{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{*}}={\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{*}}
.

Lemma 9
.

If
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{R}\subseteq{>}
then

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
.

Proof 4.2
.

According to the assumption we have
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{n}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})

for some natural number
n
. We proceed by induction on
n
. If

n=0
, the statement is trivial since
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Let
n>0

and consider
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{n-1}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
.
The induction hypothesis yields
\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{>}
. We continue
with a case analysis on the rule applied in the step

(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
. For the rules
delete
and
simplify
there
is nothing to show as the set of rewrite rules is not changed. If

deduce
is applied to a local peak there is nothing to show. Otherwise,
we have
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
where

s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t
. From the fact that
>
is

\mathcal{B}
-compatible we obtain
s>t
and therefore

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
. For
orient
we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with
s>t
, so

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
. In the case of
compose
we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\})\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow u\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}u
. Since
>
is a
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order,

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}u
implies
t>u
. From the induction hypothesis we
obtain
s>t
. Now
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
follows by the transitivity
of
>
. Finally, for
collapse
we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subsetneq\mathcal{R}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{>}
which establishes

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
.

In order to use peak-and-cliff decreasingness in the correctness proof,
we have to define an appropriate notion of labeled rewriting. Intuitively,
we want to annotate a step
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t
or
s\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
with a collection of
terms in such a way that the collection contains terms which are

\mathcal{B}
-equivalent or rewrite in a positive number of steps using
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}

to
s
and
t
, respectively.

{defi}

Let
\leftrightarrow
be a rewrite relation or equivalence relation,
M
a
finite multiset of terms and
>
a
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction
order. We write
\smash{s\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{M}t}
if
s\leftrightarrow t
and there exist
terms
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\in M
such that
s^{\prime}\gtrsim s
and
t^{\prime}\gtrsim t
for

{\gtrsim}={{>}\cup{\sim_{\mathcal{B}}}}
.

We follow the convention that if a conversion is labeled with
M
, all
single steps can be labeled with
M
.

{exa}

Consider the TRS
\mathcal{R}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+y

\displaystyle\rightarrow y

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)+y

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{s}(x+y)

and the equational theory
\mathcal{B}=\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+y\approx y+x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.
Let
{>}={\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}}
which is a
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order by
definition as
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathcal{B}
-terminating. We have

\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})+x\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})\hskip 0.85358pt\}}\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}+x)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}+x)\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}+x)\hskip 0.85358pt\}}\mathsf{s}(x)

as well as

\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})+x\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}^{*}\vphantom{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}}\mathsf{s}(x)\quad\text{but not}\quad\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})+x\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}+x)\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}^{*}\vphantom{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}+x)\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}}\mathsf{s}(x).

Lemma 10
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
.

(1)

For any finite multiset
M
we have

\smash{{\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{M}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{M}}}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}}}}
.

(2)

If
s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\>$}}]{M}t
then
s\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}^{=}\vphantom{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}}\cdot\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{N}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{N}}}t

with
\{\hskip 0.85358pts\hskip 0.85358pt\}>_{\mathsf{mul}}N
.

Proof 4.3
.

For (1) it suffices to show that

{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\>$}}]{M}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}}}
. Let

s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}\vphantom{{}^{\prime}}\>$}}]{M}t
. By definition, there exist terms
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\in M

with
s^{\prime}\gtrsim s
and
t^{\prime}\gtrsim t
. According to
Lemma
7
there exist terms
u
and
v
such that

s\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{=}u\mathrel{(\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{=}\cup\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\hskip 0.85358pt\>$}}]{*})}v\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}\>$}}]{=}t

Since
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq{>}
we have
s\gtrsim u
and
t\gtrsim v

and therefore
s^{\prime}\gtrsim u
and
t^{\prime}\gtrsim v
. Hence, every
non-empty step can be labeled with
M
and we obtain

s\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{E}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\>$}}]{M}}}t
as desired.

For a proof of (2), let
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{M}t
.
By definition, there exists an
s^{\prime}\in M

such that
s^{\prime}\gtrsim s
. We proceed by case analysis on the rule
applied in the inference step. For
deduce
,
orient
,
delete
and
simplify
there
is nothing to show since
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Suppose the step is an application of
compose
. If the rule used in the
step
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{M}t
is not altered, we are done. Otherwise, the
step was performed with a rule
{\ell\to r}\in\mathcal{R}
which is
changed to
{\ell\to r^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
with
r\rightarrow_{{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{B}}}{}r^{\prime}
.
There exist a substitution
\sigma
and a position
p
such
that
s=s[\ell\sigma]_{p}
and
t=s[r\sigma]_{p}
. The new step is

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{M}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}=s[r^{\prime}\sigma]_{p}
. Since
>
is a

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order, we have
s^{\prime}>t^{\prime}
and therefore the
label
M
is still valid. From
t^{\prime}
we can reach
t
with

t^{\prime}\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}}\cdot\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}}\cdot\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}}}}t

From
s>t
we obtain
\{\hskip 0.85358pts\hskip 0.85358pt\}>_{\mathsf{mul}}\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\hskip 0.85358pt\}
which means that the new
conversion between
s
and
t
is of the desired form.

Finally, suppose the step is an application of
collapse
. If the rule
used in the step
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{M}t
is not altered, we are done
immediately. Otherwise, the step was performed with a rule

{\ell\to r}\in\mathcal{R}
which is changed to an equation

{\ell^{\prime}\approx r}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
with
\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\ell^{\prime}
. There
exist a substitution
\sigma
and a position
p
such that

s=s[\ell\sigma]_{p}
and
t=s[r\sigma]_{p}
. The new step is

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}^{M}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}=s[\ell^{\prime}\sigma]_{p}
. Since
>
is a

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order we have
s^{\prime}>t^{\prime}
and therefore the
label
M
is still valid. From
t^{\prime}
we can reach
t
with

t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}^{N}t
where
N=\{\hskip 0.85358ptt^{\prime},t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. From
s>t^{\prime}
and
s>t

we obtain
\{\hskip 0.85358pts\hskip 0.85358pt\}>_{\mathsf{mul}}N
which means that the new conversion
between
s
and
t
is of the desired form.

Finally, we are able to prove the correctness result for
\mathsf{A}
,
i.e., all finite fair and non-failing runs produce a
\mathcal{B}
-complete
TRS which represents the original set of equations. In contrast to

[
Ave95
]
and
[
Bac91
]
, the proof shows that it suffices to
consider prime critical pairs. This is achieved by
showing peak-and-cliff decreasingness and using Theorem
2
instead
of directly using the main theorem of Section
3
(Theorem
6
).
The usage of peak-and-cliff decreasingness makes the proof more
modular and easier to formalize than the proof in
[
Ave95
]
because
it is split up into the preceding auxiliary lemmata of this
section which are mostly independent from each other and can use
different suitable proof methods. This is very different from
the approach in
[
Ave95
]
where all the necessary information and
induction hypotheses are incorporated into one large proof ordering.
The results presented in
[
HMSW19
]
for standard rewriting suggest
that our approach still has merits when compared to the original proofs
in
[
Ave95
]
and
[
Bac91
]
if infinite runs are considered.

Theorem 11
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
ES
. For every fair and non-failing run

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\cdots\,\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}

for
\mathcal{E}
, the
TRS

\mathcal{R}_{n}
is a
\mathcal{B}
-complete
representation of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof 4.4
.

Let
>
be the
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order used in the run.
From fairness we obtain
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\varnothing
as well as the fact
that
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is left-linear. establishes

{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}}^{*}}={\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}}^{*}}
and
termination modulo
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
follows from
Lemma
9
. It remains to prove that
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
which we do by showing peak-and-cliff
decreasingness. So consider a labeled local peak

t\mathrel{\prescript{M_{1}}{\mathcal{R}_{n}}{\leftarrow}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{M_{2}}u
. Lemma
5
(1)
yields
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{2}}u
. Let
v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}w
appear in this
sequence (so
v=t
or
w=u
). By definition,
v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}w
or

v\leftrightarrow_{\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}_{n}})}w
. Together with fairness, the fact that

\sim_{\mathcal{B}}
is reflexive as well as closure of rewriting under contexts
and substitutions we obtain
v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}w
or

(v,w)\in{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{R}_{i}}}}
. In both
cases, it is possible to label all steps between
v
and
w
with

\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Since
s>v
and
s>w
we have
M_{1}>_{\mathsf{mul}}\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}

and
M_{2}>_{\mathsf{mul}}\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Repeated applications of
Lemma
10
(1) therefore yield a conversion in

\mathcal{R}_{n}\cup\mathcal{B}
between
v
and
w
where every step is labeled
with a multiset that is smaller than both
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. Hence,
the corresponding condition required by peak-and-cliff
decreasingness is fulfilled.

Next consider a labeled local cliff

t\mathrel{\prescript{M_{1}}{\mathcal{R}_{n}}{\leftarrow}}s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}^{M_{2}}u
. From Lemma
5
(2)
we obtain a term
v
such that
t\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}}v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
. As in
the case for local peaks we obtain a conversion between
t
and
v

where each step can be labeled with
\{\hskip 0.85358ptt,v\hskip 0.85358pt\}<_{\mathsf{mul}}M_{1}
. Together
with fairness,
v\mathrel{\triangledown_{s}^{\smash{\sim}}}u
yields
v\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}u
or

(v,u)\in{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}}}
. In the former case
there exists a
k
such that

v\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{k}{\mathcal{R}_{n}}{\leftarrow}}u
. If
k=0

we can label all steps with
\{\hskip 0.85358ptv\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. If
k>0
the conversion is of
the form

v\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{k-1}{\mathcal{R}_{n}}{\leftarrow}}w\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{n}}$}}}$}}u
. We can label the rightmost step with
M_{2}
and the remaining
steps with
\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Note that
s>v
. Since
>
is a

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order we also have
s>w
. Thus,

M_{1}>_{\mathsf{mul}}\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
which establishes the corresponding condition
required by peak-and-cliff decreasingness for all
k
. In the
remaining case we have

(v,u)\in{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}}}
, so there is some

i\leqslant n
such that
v\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}u
. Actually, we know that

u\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}}^{M_{2}}v
since otherwise we would have both
s>v

and
v>s
by the
\mathcal{B}
-compatibility of
>
. Repeated applications
of Lemma
10
(1,2) therefore yield a conversion between

u
and
v
of the form

u\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}_{n}\>$}}]{M_{2}}}^{=}\vphantom{\xrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}_{n}\>$}}]{M_{2}}}}\cdot\mathrel{\smash{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}_{n}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{N}}^{*}\vphantom{\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}_{n}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{N}}}v

where
\{\hskip 0.85358ptu\hskip 0.85358pt\}>_{\mathsf{mul}}N
. By definition,
s^{\prime}\gtrsim u
for some

s^{\prime}\in M_{1}
and therefore
M_{1}>_{\mathsf{mul}}N
, which means that the
corresponding condition required by peak-and-cliff decreasingness is
fulfilled. Overall, it follows that
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is peak-and-cliff
decreasing and therefore Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
.

Note that the proofs of the previous theorem and Theorem
2
do not
require multiset orders induced by quasi-orders but use multiset
extensions of proper
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction orders which are
easier to work with. This could be achieved by defining peak-and-cliff
decreasingness in such a way that well-founded orders suffice for the
abstract setting. However, the usage of multiset orders based on

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction orders as well as a notion of labeled
rewriting which allows us to label steps with
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent terms
are crucial in order to establish peak-and-cliff decreasingness for
TRSs.

As we have established correctness of
\mathsf{A}
, it is natural to
ask the question whether
\mathsf{A}
is also
complete
, i.e.,
can
\mathsf{A}
generate a complete presentation whenever there exists one.
Contrary to ordered completion where there are known completeness
results (see e.g.
[
BDP89
,
Dev91
]
),
this is not possible in our setting.
Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}

[
BD94
]
consisting of the three equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\cdot(-x+x)

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\cdot(x+-x)

\displaystyle\approx x+-x

\displaystyle-x+x

\displaystyle\approx y+-y

which admits the following complete presentation
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\cdot\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle x+-x

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle-x+x

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{0}

In standard completion, only the first two equations can be
oriented from left to right but no further step is possible. In
\mathsf{A}
,
we have the same situation if
\mathcal{B}=\varnothing
, but we can choose

\mathcal{B}
as some nonempty subset of
\mathcal{E}
. Note that the first and
third equation are not eligible since they violate the assumption

\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({\ell})=\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({r})
. Hence,

\mathcal{B}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{1}\cdot(x+-x)\approx x+-x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
is the only other option.
After orienting the first equation, again no further step is possible
since there is also no overlap between
\mathsf{1}\cdot(-x+x)
and

x+-x
.

5.
Bachmair’s Inference System

As already mentioned, the inference system proposed by Avenhaus

[
Ave95
]
is essentially the same as
\mathsf{A}
. The only other inference
system for
\mathcal{B}
-completion for left-linear TRSs is due to Bachmair

[
Bac91
]
. We investigate a slightly modified version of this
inference system where arbitrary local peaks are deducible and the
encompassment condition from the
collapse
rule is removed as we only
consider finite runs. The resulting system will be called
\mathsf{B}
.
Note that the purpose of the change in the
deduce
rule of Bachmair’s
system is to eliminate this unimportant difference to
\mathsf{A}
. The following
results would still hold if we limited
deduce
in
\mathsf{B}
to (prime) critical
pairs but aligning
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
would be unnecessarily complicated.
Furthermore, deducing arbitrary local peaks offers a simpler definition
of
deduce
which gives implementations more freedom. However, we are not
aware of any work that has investigated whether deducing non-critical
peaks can be beneficial in completion.

The main difference between
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
is that in
\mathsf{B}
one may only
use the standard rewrite relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
for simplifying equations
and composing rules. This allows us to deduce local cliffs as
equations. The goal of this section is to establish correctness of

\mathsf{B}
via a simulation by
\mathsf{A}
.

{defi}

The inference system
\mathsf{B}
is the same as
\mathsf{A}
but with
rewriting in
compose
and
simplify
restricted to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
and the
following rule which replaces the two deduction rules of
\mathsf{A}
:

\textsf{deduce}\quad\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}\quad\text{if $s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}^{\pm}}t$}

{exa}

Recall the ES
\mathcal{E}=\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{0}\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
of Example
4.1
,
where
+
is an AC function symbol. AC completion based on
\mathsf{B}
proceeds as follows:

(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,(\varnothing,\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x\hskip 0.85358pt\})\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,(\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{0}+x\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\},\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x\hskip 0.85358pt\})\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,\cdots

The second step obtained by
deduce
reveals a
main difference between
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
. If
deduce
in
\mathsf{A}
is employed,

(\varnothing,\{\hskip 0.85358ptx+\mathsf{0}\rightarrow x\hskip 0.85358pt\})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\varnothing,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{0}+x\to x,\;\mathsf{0}+x\rightarrow x\hskip 0.85358pt\})

is obtained.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an ES. A finite sequence

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,\cdots\,\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}

with
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\varnothing
is a
run
for

\mathcal{E}
. If
\mathcal{E}_{n}\neq\varnothing
, the run
fails
. The run
is
fair
if
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is left-linear and the following inclusion
holds:

\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}_{n}})\cup{\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})}\,\subseteq\,{{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}}^{\sim}}\cup{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}}}}

In contrast to Definition
4.1
, the fairness condition is the same
for all prime critical pairs since the inference rule
deduce
of

\mathsf{B}
never produces rewrite rules. In that sense,
\mathsf{B}
is closer to
known completion procedures, but as shown before, this comes at the expense
of not being allowed to apply
simplify
,
collapse
and
compose
with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
.
If we want to allow this more general kind of simplification as it is the
case for
\mathsf{A}
, local cliffs have to be deduced as rules. Note that this
possibly leads to an increase in critical pairs which one has to consider
in practice, but it can also reduce the number of
orient
steps one has to
perform. Since
compose
and
collapse
(without the encompassment condition) can
emulate the behavior of
simplify
, it is possible to get the best of both
worlds by using
\mathsf{A}
and deferring the computation of
critical pairs with rules which stem from local cliffs until it is
needed to proceed in the completion process. We will now show that
for finite runs,
\mathsf{A}
is at least as powerful as
\mathsf{B}
which is the main
motivation for the focus on
\mathsf{A}
instead of
\mathsf{B}
in this article. Moreover,
the simulation result actually allows us to reduce correctness of
\mathsf{B}
to
correctness of
\mathsf{A}
, so we get this property without any additional effort.

In order to prove that fair and non-failing runs in
\mathsf{B}
can be simulated
in
\mathsf{A}
, we start with the following technical lemma which intuitively
states that a step in
\mathsf{B}
can be simulated by at most one step in

\mathsf{A}
in such a way that the results only differ in the number of oriented
equations.
We denote an application of the rule
orient
in an inference system
I
by
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize{I} }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle\phantom{*}$}}}
.

Lemma 12
.

If
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})
then
there exists a pair
(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
such that

(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{=}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
.
In a picture:
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}
\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2}
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime}
\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}
\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{=}

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}

Proof 5.1
.

Let
>
be a fixed
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order which is used in
both
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
. From

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})
we
obtain
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{E}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime-1}
. In order
to simplify the formulation, we will refer to the sequence of

orient
steps between a pair and its primed variant as the invariant.
We proceed by a case analysis on the rule applied in the inference
step
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
.

\triangleright

In the case of
deduce
, we apply the same rule in
\mathsf{A}
. For
local peaks
s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{1}}$}}}$}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}t
we have

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
,

\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
. For
local cliffs
s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{1}}$}}}$}}\cdot\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{B}}t
we have

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{1}
as well as

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\rightarrow s\hskip 0.85358pt\}
,
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
. In both
cases, the invariant is preserved.

\triangleright

Suppose the inference step in
\mathsf{B}
orients an equation
s\approx t
. If

{s\approx t}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
we perform the same
step in
\mathsf{A}
which preserves the invariant. Otherwise,

{s\approx t}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}
and hence

{v\rightarrow w}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
where
\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}=\{\hskip 0.85358pts,t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. In this case, an
empty step in
\mathsf{A}
preserves the invariant.

\triangleright

If the inference step in
\mathsf{B}
deletes an equation

s\approx t
, it has to be in
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
which enables us to
perform the same step in
\mathsf{A}
while preserving the invariant:
Suppose that
{s\approx t}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
. Since
the equation is deleted, we have
s\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
. Neither
s>t
nor

t>s
can hold as
>
is
\mathcal{B}
-compatible and irreflexive. Hence, the
equation cannot be oriented which contradicts the assumption

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})
.

\triangleright

If the inference step in
\mathsf{B}
is
compose
or
collapse
, the same
step can be performed in
\mathsf{A}
while preserving the invariant as

\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
.

\triangleright

Finally, suppose that the inference step in

\mathsf{B}
simplifies an equation
s\approx t
. Since the
orientation of equations does not matter in completion, we may
assume that the simplification transforms the equation to

s^{\prime}\approx t
without loss of generality. If

s\approx t\in\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime}
, we perform the same step in

\mathsf{A}
which preserves the invariant. Otherwise, there is a rule

{v\rightarrow w}\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}=\{\hskip 0.85358pts,t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. If
v=s

and
w=t
, we use
collapse
for the inference step in
\mathsf{A}
which
produces the same equation
{s^{\prime}\approx t}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}
which means
that the invariant is preserved. If
v=t
and
w=s
, we use

compose
for the inference step in
\mathsf{A}
in order to obtain the rule

t\rightarrow s^{\prime}
. From
t>s
and
s>s^{\prime}
we obtain
t>s^{\prime}
and
therefore the equation
s^{\prime}\approx t
can be oriented into the
rule
t\rightarrow s^{\prime}
. Thus, the invariant is preserved.

For the proof of the simulation result, we need a slightly different
form of the previous lemma. Analogous to the notation for rewrite
relations, the relation
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize{I} }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}
denotes
the exhaustive application of the inference rule
orient
.

Corollary 13
.

If
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})
then

(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
where

(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
.

Proof 5.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})
.
Lemma
12
yields
(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{=}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})
. It
follows that the orientable equations in
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}
are also included
in
\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Hence, we can compute

(\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{3}^{\prime})
,
satisfying
(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{3}^{\prime})
and

(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{3}^{\prime})
as
desired.

Theorem 14
.

For every fair run
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}^{*}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R})

there exists a fair run
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R})
.

Proof 5.3
.

Assume
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0})\vdash_{\mathsf{B}}^{n}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n})
where

\mathcal{R}_{0}=\mathcal{E}_{n}=\varnothing
. By
n
applications of
we arrive at the following situation:
\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{0}^{\prime}
\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
\cdots
\cdots
\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}
\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}

The following two statements hold:

(1)

For
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, all orientable equations in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
are
in
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}
(possibly reversed) and the other equations are in

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\prime}
.

(2)

\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
is a set of orientable equations.

Statement (1) is immediate from the simulation relation

\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\>\text{{o}}\>\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle!$}}}
and statement (2) follows from

\mathcal{B}
-compatibility of the used reduction order together with the
fact that every (prime) critical pair is connected by one

\mathcal{R}_{n}
-step and one
\mathcal{B}
-step. Furthermore,
\mathcal{E}_{n}=\varnothing

implies
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{\prime}=\varnothing
as well as
\mathcal{R}_{n}=\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}
. Hence,
we obtain fairness of the run in
\mathsf{A}
by showing the following
inclusions:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\sim}}\cup{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,{{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\sim}}\cup{\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\prime}}}}}

Let
{s\approx t}\in\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}})
. By fairness of the run in

\mathsf{B}
we obtain
s\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\sim}t
or
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}t
for
some
k\leqslant n
. In the former case, we are immediately done. In
the latter case we obtain
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\prime}}t
from (1)
as desired. Now, let
{s\approx t}\in\mathsf{PCP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\pm}})
. By
fairness of the run in
\mathsf{B}
we obtain
s\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\sim}t

or
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}t
for some
k\leqslant n
. Again, we are immediately
done in the former case. In the latter case we have

s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\prime}}t
because of (1) and (2). Therefore, the run in

\mathsf{A}
is fair.

The previous theorem is an important simulation result which justifies
the emphasis on
\mathsf{A}
in this article. Moreover, together with
Theorem
11
the correctness of the inference system

\mathsf{B}
is an easy consequence.

Corollary 15
.

Every fair and non-failing run for
\mathcal{E}
in
\mathsf{B}
produces a

\mathcal{B}
-complete presentation of
\mathcal{E}
.

6.
Canonicity

Complete representations resulting from completion may have redundant
rules which do not contribute to the computation of normal forms. The
notion of
canonicity
addresses this issue by defining a minimal
and unique representation of a complete TRS for a given reduction
order. In this section, canonicity results for
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRSs are
presented. After establishing results for abstract rewriting, means
to compute
\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRSs and the uniqueness of
\mathcal{B}
-canonical
TRSs are discussed. The results and proofs in this section closely
follow the presentation of canonicity results for standard rewriting
in
[
HMSW19
]
by carefully lifting definitions and results to
rewriting modulo
\mathcal{B}
. To the best of our knowledge, this section
presents the first account of canonicity results for
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRSs.

6.1.
Results for Abstract Rewriting Modulo

In the following we assume that

\mathcal{A}_{1}=\langle A,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\rangle
and

\mathcal{A}_{2}=\langle A,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}\rangle
are ARSs
with the same underlying set
A

and
\sim
is an
equivalence relation on

A
.
The upcoming
definition introduces two different notions of equivalence
between ARSs. Both
are needed in order to relate
different representations of the same equational theory,
in particular the relation between complete presentations
and their canonical forms.

{defi}

The ARSs
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are

(conversion) equivalent modulo
\sim
if

{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{*}}={\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{*}}

and
normalization equivalent modulo
\sim
if

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim}={\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim}
.

{exa}

Let
A=\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c}\hskip 0.85358pt\}
,
\mathsf{a}\sim\mathsf{b}
,

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c})\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c})\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.
Then,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are conversion equivalent modulo

\sim
but not normalization equivalent modulo
\sim
:
We have
\mathsf{a}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\mathsf{c}
but
\mathsf{a}\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}_{2}})

and
\mathsf{a}\not\sim\mathsf{c}
.

Next we show two fundamental properties of normalization
equivalence modulo
\sim
which are needed in Theorem
19
.

Lemma 16
.

If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}

are normalization equivalent modulo
\sim
and terminating, they are
equivalent modulo
\sim
.

Proof 6.1
.

We prove
{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{*}}\subseteq{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{*}}
by
induction on the length of conversions in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. The claim follows
by symmetry. If
a\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{0}b
then
a=b
and therefore
also
a\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{0}b
. For

a\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{n}a^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}b
the induction hypothesis
yields
a\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{*}a^{\prime}
. If
a^{\prime}\sim b
, the result follows
immediately. Otherwise, either
a^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}b
or

b\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}a^{\prime}
. We only consider the first case by again
exploiting symmetry. Since
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are normalization
equivalent and terminating, there is an element
c
such that

b\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim c
and
b\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim c

and therefore also
a^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim c
and

a^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim c
. Hence, we can connect
a^{\prime}
and
b

by a conversion in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
as desired.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}_{2}})\subseteq\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}_{1}})
and

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\subseteq{({\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}}/{\sim})^{+}}
. If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is
complete modulo
\sim
then
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is complete modulo
\sim
and
normalization equivalent modulo
\sim
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

Proof 6.2
.

From the inclusion

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\subseteq{({\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}}/{\sim})^{+}}
as well as the
termination of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
modulo
\sim
we obtain that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
terminating modulo
\sim
. Next, we show

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim}
. The inclusion

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\subseteq{({\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}}/{\sim})^{+}}
gives rise to

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}}\subseteq{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{*}}
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{1}

is Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
and
\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}_{2}})\subseteq\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{A}_{1}})

we have
{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim}

and therefore

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim}

as desired. For the reverse inclusion
consider
a\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}b^{\prime}\sim b
. The termination of
\mathcal{A}_{2}

modulo
\sim
implies the termination of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, so there is some

c
such that
a\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim c
and thus

a\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}c^{\prime}\sim c
. Now, the Church–Rosser modulo
\sim

property of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
yields
b^{\prime}\sim c^{\prime}
. We obtain
b\sim c
and
therefore
a\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim b
. Hence,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and

\mathcal{A}_{2}
are normalization equivalent modulo
\sim
. Finally, the
Church–Rosser modulo
\sim
property of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
follows from the
sequence of inclusions

{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{*}}\,\subseteq\,{\Leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{*}}\,\subseteq\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}^{!}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{!}{\mathcal{A}_{1}}{\leftarrow}}}\,\subseteq\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}^{!}\cdot\sim\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{!}{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\leftarrow}}}

which are justified by

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\subseteq{({\rightarrow_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}}/{\sim})^{+}}
, the fact that

\mathcal{A}_{1}
is complete modulo
\sim
and normalization equivalence
modulo
\sim
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, respectively.

6.2.
Computing
\mathcal{B}
-Canonical Term Rewrite
Systems

Intuitively,
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRSs need to have a variety of left-hand
sides of rules in order to match every possible
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent term
of a reducible term. However, for the right-hand sides of rules only
one representative of the
\mathcal{B}
-equivalence class suffices. This
rationale is reflected in the following series of definitions.

{defi}

Two rules
\ell\rightarrow r
and
\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow r^{\prime}
are

right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants
if there exists a renaming

\sigma
such that
\ell\sigma=\ell^{\prime}
and
r\sigma\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r^{\prime}
. We
write
\mathcal{R}_{1}\doteq_{\sim}\mathcal{R}_{2}
if every rule of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
has a
right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variant in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
and vice versa. For any
TRS
\mathcal{R}
, the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{\doteq_{\sim}}
denotes a right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent
variant-free version, i.e., only one representative of every
equivalence class of right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants is present.

{exa}

From the TRS
\mathcal{R}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)\times y

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(x\times y)+y

\displaystyle y\times\mathsf{s}(x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(x\times y)+y

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)\times y

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,y+(x\times y)

where
+
and
\times
are AC function symbols we obtain

\mathcal{R}_{\doteq_{\sim}}
by removing the third rule:

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)\times y

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(x\times y)+y

\displaystyle y\times\mathsf{s}(x)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(x\times y)+y

The relation
\doteq_{\sim}
weakens the notion of literal similarity of
TRSs (
\doteq
) to the setting of the inference system
\mathsf{A}
: While
composing with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
is allowed, left-hand sides may only be rewritten
with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
.

{defi}

A TRS
\mathcal{R}
is
left-reduced
if for every rewrite rule

\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
,
\ell
is a normal form of

\mathcal{R}\setminus\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced
if for every rewrite rule

{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\mathcal{R}
,
r
is a normal form with respect to

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
. We say that
\mathcal{R}
is
canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}

if it is complete modulo
\mathcal{B}
, left-reduced and right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced.

For standard rewriting, canonical systems are defined in
[
Mét83
]

as TRSs which are complete, left-reduced and
right-reduced
. Here,
right-reduced is defined just as right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced but with

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
instead of
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
. Intuitively, canonical systems are minimal
complete systems because the rules cannot be used to simplify (other) rules
anymore. In our setting, it is important to note that while right-hand
sides can be simplified with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
, for left-hand sides
only simplification with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
is considered. This ensures
that all terms which are reducible with respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
can also be
rewritten with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Note that this also reflects the definition of

collapse
and
compose
in
\mathsf{A}
.

The next definition is an extension of Métivier’s proposed procedure

[
Mét83
, Theorem 7]
to transform a complete system into a canonical
system. As in the original definition, a
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRS is transformed
into a
\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRSs in two stages: First, a right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced
version without right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants is constructed. After
that, superfluous rules are removed in order to obtain a left-reduced
system. Hence, the overall result is canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}
. Needless to
say, this is only computable if
\mathcal{B}
has a decidable equational theory. A
presentation of Métivier’s concept for standard rewriting in the style
of our upcoming definition can be found in
[
HMSW19
]
.

{defi}

Given a
\mathcal{B}
-terminating TRS
\mathcal{R}
, the TRSs
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
and

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}}\mid{\ell\to r}\in\mathcal{R}\hskip 0.85358pt\}_{\doteq_{\sim}}

\displaystyle\ddot{\mathcal{R}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r\in\dot{\mathcal{R}}\mid\ell\in\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}\setminus\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r\hskip 0.85358pt\}})\hskip 0.85358pt\}

Here,
r{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}}
denotes an arbitrary normal form of
r
with
respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
.

{exa}

Consider again the ES
\mathcal{E}
from Example
4.1
.
If neither
collapse
nor
compose
is used, AC completion results in the
complete presentation
\mathcal{R}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime},4\,\text{--}\,10,13,14,15\hskip 0.85358pt\}
.
Because the right-hand sides of
14
and
15
are reducible by

\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}
and also because
13
is a right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variant of

1^{\prime}
,
the TRS
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
consists of
1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime},4\,\text{--}\,10
and the
modified rules:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+(y+z))

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 14^{\prime\prime}$}\,}\,(\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y))+\mathsf{f}(z)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}((x+y)+z)

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 15^{\prime\prime}$}\,}\,\mathsf{f}(x)+(\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(z))

Since the left-hand sides of
5\,\text{--}\,10
,
14^{\prime\prime}
, and
15^{\prime\prime}
are
reducible by
1^{\prime}
,
3^{\prime}
or
4
, they are excluded from
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
. Thus,

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt1^{\prime},2^{\prime},3^{\prime},4\hskip 0.85358pt\}
is obtained.
As expected, it is the result of performing completion with
collapse
and
compose
.

The following example shows that for preserving the equational theory,
it does not suffice to make
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
variant-free
in the sense of
\doteq
.

{exa}

Suppose we change the definition to

\dot{\mathcal{R}}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r{\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}}\mid{\ell\to r}\in\mathcal{R}\hskip 0.85358pt\}

where variant-freeness is implicit. Consider the TRS

\mathcal{R}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x+y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(x)

where
+
is an AC function symbol. We have
\dot{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{R}

since the two rules are not variants and therefore

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}=\varnothing
which obviously induces a
different equational theory than
\mathcal{R}
. However, by using
Definition
6.2
we obtain e.g. the following
\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRS:

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}\,=\,\dot{\mathcal{R}}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{f}(x+y)\rightarrow\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)\hskip 0.85358pt\}

Before we can prove the main result of this section, we need the
following technical lemma.

Lemma 18
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{B}
-complete
TRS
and
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
then

\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})\subseteq\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})
.

Proof 6.3
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathcal{B}
-complete and
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
. We prove the contrapositive

s\notin\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})\implies s\notin\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})

for which it suffices to show that

\ell\notin\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})
whenever

{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\dot{\mathcal{R}}
due to closure of rewriting under
contexts and substitutions. We prove this by induction on
\ell

with respect to the well-founded order
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
. If

{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
, the claim follows
immediately. Otherwise,

\ell\notin\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}\setminus\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\rightarrow r\hskip 0.85358pt\}})
, i.e., there
is a rule
{\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow r^{\prime}}\in\dot{\mathcal{R}}
with
\ell\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}\ell^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the rules
\ell\rightarrow r
and
\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow r
are not
right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants by the definition of

\dot{\mathcal{R}}
. By definition,

{\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}}={\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}}\cup{\doteq}
, so we continue by a case analysis
on
\ell\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}\ell^{\prime}
.

\triangleright

If
\ell\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}\ell^{\prime}
, the induction hypothesis yields

\ell^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})
and therefore

\ell\notin\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})
as desired.

\triangleright

If
\ell\doteq\ell^{\prime}
then by definition there exists a renaming

\sigma
such that
\ell=\ell^{\prime}\sigma
. From the right-
\mathcal{B}
-reducedness
of
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
we conclude that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are normal forms with
respect to
\rightarrow_{\dot{\mathcal{R}}/\mathcal{B}}
. Since normal forms are closed
under renamings, this also holds for
r^{\prime}\sigma
. Together with

r\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\dot{\mathcal{R}}}$}}}$}}\ell=\ell^{\prime}\sigma\rightarrow_{\dot{\mathcal{R}}}r^{\prime}\sigma

and the Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
property of
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
we
obtain
r\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r^{\prime}\sigma
. Thus,
\ell\rightarrow r
and
\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow r^{\prime}
are
right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants. This contradicts the definition
of
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
. Therefore, this case cannot happen.

Theorem 19
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{B}
-complete
TRS
then
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
is a

TRS
which is normalization equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
,
conversion equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
and canonical modulo

\mathcal{B}
.

Proof 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRS. By definition,

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}
. Since
\mathcal{R}
and
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
have the same
left-hand sides, their normal forms coincide. An application of
Lemma
17
yields that
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
is

\mathcal{B}
-complete and normalization equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
.
Furthermore,
\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})=\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})
: The
inclusion
\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})\subseteq\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})

follows from Lemma
18
and the inclusion

\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})\subseteq\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})
is a consequence
of
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{R}}
. Now, another
application of Lemma
17
yields that

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
is also
\mathcal{B}
-complete and normalization
equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
. The TRS
\dot{\mathcal{R}}
is
right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced by definition. Since

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{R}}
,
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
is
also right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced. Together with the already established fact
that
\mathsf{NF}({\dot{\mathcal{R}}})=\mathsf{NF}({\ddot{\mathcal{R}}})
, left-reducedness
of
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
follows from its definition. Hence,

\ddot{\mathcal{R}}
is canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}
. Finally,
Lemma
16
establishes that
\ddot{\mathcal{R}}

is not only normalization equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
but also
(conversion) equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
.

Due to the constructive nature of Definition
6.2
, the
previous theorem states that given a
\mathcal{B}
-complete TRS
\mathcal{R}
it is
possible to compute (if the equational theory of
\mathcal{B}
is decidable)
a
\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRS which is equivalent modulo

\mathcal{B}
to
\mathcal{R}
. An inspection of Definition
6.2

further reveals that the inference system
\mathsf{A}
can produce

\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRSs: If

(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R})
is a fair run
and neither
compose
nor
collapse
are applicable, then
\mathcal{R}
is not only
complete but also canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}
. The following lemma shows that
this also holds for the inference system
\mathsf{B}
.

Lemma 20
.

If
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{B}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R})
is a fair
run and neither
compose
nor
collapse
are applicable, then
\mathcal{R}
is

\mathcal{B}
-canonical.

Proof 6.5
.

According to , the TRS
\mathcal{R}

is a
\mathcal{B}
-complete presentation of
\mathcal{E}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}
is
left-reduced by definition as
collapse
is not applicable. For a proof
by contradiction, suppose that
\mathcal{R}
is not
right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced. Hence, there exists a rule
\ell\rightarrow r
in
\mathcal{R}

which can be modified to
\ell\rightarrow r^{\prime}
where

r\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}^{!}r^{\prime}
.
Note that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are not equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
as
\mathcal{R}

is terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}
. From the
\mathcal{B}
-completeness of
\mathcal{R}

we obtain
r\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{!}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}r^{\prime}
.
Since
r^{\prime}
is a normal form with respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
, we
have
r\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r^{\prime}
which contradicts our
assumption that
compose
is not applicable.

Therefore, both
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
can produce canonical systems due to
the availability of
compose
and
collapse
which is also referred to as

inter-reduction
. If a given completion procedure lacks
inter-reduction, it is an instance of
elementary completion
.
Note that the original completion procedure by Knuth and
Bendix
[
KB70
]
performs elementary completion. The next example
shows that there is a big difference between
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
with
respect to elementary completion. In particular,
\mathsf{A}
cannot
simulate
\mathsf{B}
if both are restricted to elementary completion:
In order to simulate
\mathsf{B}
’s
simplify
in
\mathsf{A}
,
compose
and
collapse
are needed as can be seen in the proof of Lemma
12
.

{exa}

Consider the ESs
\mathcal{E}=\{\hskip 0.85358ptx\cdot\mathsf{1}\approx x\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

\mathcal{B}=\{\hskip 0.85358pt(x\cdot y)\cdot z\approx x\cdot(y\cdot z)\hskip 0.85358pt\}

as well as a corresponding run in
\mathsf{A}
. There is only one option to
orient the only equation into a terminating rewrite rule, namely

x\cdot\mathsf{1}\rightarrow x
. From the critical peak

x\cdot y\xleftarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{\epsilon}(x\cdot y)\cdot\mathsf{1}\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\>$}}]{\epsilon}x\cdot(y\cdot 1)
we can deduce the rule

x\cdot(y\cdot 1)\rightarrow x\cdot y
. In
\mathsf{B}
restricted to elementary
completion, the result of
deduce
would be the corresponding equation
which can be simplified to a trivial equation and therefore
deleted. In
\mathsf{A}
restricted to elementary completion, however,
there are no means of removing or even altering the deduced
rule. Hence, the rule will be used to deduce more critical pairs
with the associativity axiom which are again kept as
rules. Clearly, this process cannot terminate as overlaps with the
associativity axioms can increase the size of the left-hand side
of the rules without bound.

The previous example shows that for many ESs, there will not
be a finite run in
\mathsf{A}
restricted to elementary completion. Hence,
the usage of inter-reduction and the resulting definition of
canonicity is crucial for the success of
\mathsf{A}
in generating finite
solutions to validity problems.
We close the section by stating the fact that even for reduced TRSs
which are not complete, there may be fewer prime critical pairs
than ordinary critical pairs. In Examples
3
and
3.2

we already observed that for a complete TRS.

{exa}

Consider the left-linear reduced TRS
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(x,\mathsf{a}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow x

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},x))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{a}

The TRS admits the three critical pairs stemming from the following critical peaks:

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}

\underline{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}))}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{a}\vphantom{|}

\mathsf{f}(\underline{\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\vphantom{|}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))\vphantom{|}

As the first two are not prime,
\mathsf{PCP}({\mathcal{R}})
only consists of the two
equations
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\approx\mathsf{a}
and

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))\approx\mathsf{a}
. Note that the TRS is completed by
adding the rule
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\to\mathsf{a}
corresponding to the former
equation.

6.3.
Uniqueness of
\mathcal{B}
-Canonical Term
Rewrite Systems

The main result of this section states that
\mathcal{B}
-canonical TRSs which
are compatible with the same
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order are
unique up to right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants. This property justifies
the usage of the term
canonical
and motivates the computation
of TRSs which are canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}
as discussed in the last
section. In order to prove this result, we start with an auxiliary
lemma.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a
TRS
which is right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced and let
s

be a reducible term which is minimal with respect to
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
. If

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
then
s\rightarrow t
is a
right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variant of a rule in
\mathcal{R}
.

Proof 6.6
.

Let
\ell\rightarrow r
be the first rule which is applied in the sequence

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
, so
s\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\trianglerighteq}}\ell
. Since
s
is
a minimal reducible term with respect to
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
, we have

s\doteq\ell
. By definition, there is a renaming
\sigma
such
that
s=\ell\sigma
. Since
\mathcal{R}
is right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced,
r
is a
normal form with respect to
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}}
and therefore also

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
. Closure of normal forms under renamings yields

r\sigma\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{R}})
. Hence,
t\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r\sigma
and we conclude
that
s\rightarrow t
and
\ell\rightarrow r
are right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants.

In contrast to its counterpart for standard rewriting in

[
HMSW19
, Theorem 4.9]
, the following lemma needs termination modulo

\mathcal{B}
as an
additional precondition. Note that it still treats a more general case
than the main result of this subsection (Theorem
23
) as
the Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
property is not required.

Lemma 22
.

TRS
s which are
normalization equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
, terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}
,
left-reduced and right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced are unique up to
right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants.

Proof 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
be TRSs which are normalization equivalent
modulo
\mathcal{B}
, terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}
, left-reduced and
right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced. As the argument is symmetric, we only show that
every rule of
\mathcal{R}
has a right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variant in

\mathcal{S}
. Consider
{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\mathcal{R}
. Note that
\ell
and
r
are
not
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent since otherwise the cycle

r\sim_{\mathcal{B}}\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}r
contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{R}
is
terminating modulo
\mathcal{B}
. Furthermore,
r\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{R}})
as
\mathcal{R}
is
right-
\mathcal{B}
-reduced. Hence, normalization equivalence modulo
\mathcal{B}

of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
yields
\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{+}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r
. Moreover, the left-reducedness of
\mathcal{R}
yields that
\ell
is a
minimal
\mathcal{R}
-reducible term with respect to
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
. Now suppose
there exists a rule
{\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow r^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}
such that

\ell\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}\ell^{\prime}
. Right-
\mathcal{B}
-reducedness of
\mathcal{S}
yields

r^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{S}})
and termination modulo
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{S}
implies that

\ell^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
are not
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent. Together with
normalization equivalence modulo
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
we obtain

\ell^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}r^{\prime}
which contradicts the fact
that
\ell
is a minimal
\mathcal{R}
-reducible term with respect to

\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
. Therefore,
\ell
is a minimal
\mathcal{S}
-reducible term with
respect to
\mathrel{\makebox[0.0pt]{\makebox[9.0pt][r]{\lower 0.1pt\hbox{$\cdot$}}}{\vartriangleright}}
and from Lemma
21
we obtain that

\ell\rightarrow r
is a right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variant of a rule in
\mathcal{S}

which concludes the proof.

In the proof of the following theorem we now just need to establish
the preconditions of the previous lemma.

Theorem 23
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
be
TRS
s which are equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
and
canonical modulo
\mathcal{B}
. If
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
are compatible with the same

\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order then
\mathcal{R}\doteq_{\sim}\mathcal{S}
.

Proof 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
be compatible with the
\mathcal{B}
-compatible
reduction order
>
. We show that
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
are normalization
equivalent modulo
\mathcal{B}
which allows us to conclude the proof by
Lemma
22
. As the argument is symmetric,
we only show

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}}
.
Consider
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}t^{\prime}\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
. Since
\mathcal{S}

is terminating, there exists a term
u
such that

t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}u
. From the equivalence modulo
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{R}
and

\mathcal{S}
we obtain
t^{\prime}\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{R}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{*}u
. As
\mathcal{R}
is
Church–Rosser modulo
\mathcal{B}
and
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{R}})
we have

u\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}
. If
t^{\prime}
and
u
are not

\mathcal{B}
-equivalent then both
u>t^{\prime}
(as

u\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}
) and
t^{\prime}>u
(as
t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}u
),
which contradicts the irreflexivity of
>
.
If
t^{\prime}\neq u
, we also obtain a contradiction to the irreflexivity
of
>
from
u\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}u
together with termination
modulo
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{S}
. Hence,
t^{\prime}=u
which means that

t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{S}})
. Equivalence of
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
modulo
\mathcal{B}

yields
s\xleftrightarrow[\smash{\raisebox{0.56905pt}{$\scriptstyle\>\mathcal{S}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathcal{B}\>$}}]{*}t^{\prime}
from which we obtain

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}
by
\mathcal{B}
-completeness of
\mathcal{S}

and
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{S}})
. Finally,
t\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}
establishes

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}^{!}\cdot\sim_{\mathcal{B}}t
as desired.

The previous result cannot be strengthened to literal similarity as
the following counterexample shows.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
consisting of the single equation

\mathsf{f}(x+y)\,\approx\,\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)

where
+
is an AC function symbol. There are two
\mathcal{B}
-complete
presentations of
\mathcal{E}
consisting of one rule each:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{f}(x+y)\rightarrow\mathsf{f}(x)+\mathsf{f}(y)\hskip 0.85358pt\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\,=\,\{\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{f}(x+y)\rightarrow\mathsf{f}(y)+\mathsf{f}(x)\hskip 0.85358pt\}

While the rules of the two TRSs are right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants,
they are not variants. Note that both systems are compatible with
the same
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order.

Note that
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
in the previous example can also be
obtained with the inference system
\mathsf{B}
. This shows that while the
relation
\doteq_{\sim}
is motivated by the definition of the inference
system
\mathsf{A}
, the notion of right-
\mathcal{B}
-equivalent variants
naturally arises in completion modulo
\mathcal{B}
for left-linear TRSs.

7.
AC Completion

So far, the theoretical results have been generalized by using
an arbitrary equational theory
\mathcal{B}
. In practice, however, this
article is concerned with the particular theory AC. The results of
this section allow us to assess the effectiveness of the inference system

\mathsf{A}
in the setting of AC completion.

7.1.
Limitations of Left-Linear AC Completion

In addition to the restriction to left-linear rewrite rules, the
following example demonstrates another severe limitation of the
inference system
\mathsf{A}
previously unmentioned in the literature.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
consisting of the equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{1},\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

where
\mathsf{and}
is an AC function symbol. There is only one way to
orient each equation. Furthermore, there are no critical pairs
between the resulting rewrite rules. Hence, using the inference
system
\mathsf{A}
we arrive at the intermediate TRS

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{1},\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{0}

where the only possible next step is to deduce local cliffs. We will
now show that this has to be done infinitely many times. Note that
an AC-complete presentation
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathcal{E}
has to be able to rewrite
any term that is AC-equivalent to a reducible term. Consider the
infinite family of terms

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},x_{1}),\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},x_{1}),x_{2}),\mathsf{1})

\displaystyle\cdots

as well as

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},x_{1})

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},x_{1}),x_{2})

\displaystyle\cdots

Clearly,
s_{n}\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}t_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

and therefore also
s_{n}\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}t_{n}
for all

n\in\mathbb{N}
, but this demands infinitely many rules in
\mathcal{R}
:
For each
s_{n}
there is an AC-equivalent term such that the
constants
\mathsf{0}
and
\mathsf{1}
are next to each other which allows
us to rewrite it using the rule
\mathsf{and}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{1})\rightarrow\mathsf{0}
. However,
with
n
also the number of variables between
these constants increases which requires
\mathcal{R}
to have infinitely
many rules since rewrite rules can only be applied before the
representation modulo AC is changed.

Note that there is nothing special about this example except the fact
that it contains at least one equation which can only be oriented such
that the left-hand side contains an AC function symbol where both
arguments have “structure”, i.e., both arguments contain a
function symbol which is different from the original AC function
symbol. As a consequence, the necessity of infinitely many rules
applies
to all equational systems which have this property. Needless to say,
this means that for a large class of equational systems the
corresponding AC-canonical presentation (in the left-linear sense) is
infinite if it exists. This observation is in stark contrast to the
