properties of general AC completion as presented in the next section
which can complete the ES
\mathcal{E}
from Example
7.1
into a finite
AC-canonical TRS by simply orienting all rules from left to
right. The following example shows that given a nonempty context,
the same effect can also be seen even when only one argument
contains a different function symbol.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
consisting of the equation

\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{p}(x)+y)=x+y
where
+
is an AC function symbol.
Note that orienting the equation from left to right is the only
way to convert
\mathcal{E}
into a terminating TRS
\mathcal{R}
. The rule has
no critical pairs with itself, but as in the previous example,
adding critical pairs with
\mathsf{AC}
does not terminate. This can
be seen by considering the infinite family of terms

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{p}(x)+y_{1})

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{s}((\mathsf{p}(x)+y_{1})+y_{2})

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle\,=\,\mathsf{s}(((\mathsf{p}(x)+y_{1})+y_{2})+y_{3})

\displaystyle\cdots

as well as

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle\,=\,x+y_{1}

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle\,=\,(x+y_{1})+y_{2}

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle\,=\,((x+y_{1})+y_{2})+y_{3}

\displaystyle\cdots

Again,
s_{n}\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}t_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For a complete presentation
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathcal{E}\cup\mathsf{AC}
we have

s_{n}\downarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{\sim}t_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, but this
demands infinitely many rules in
\mathcal{R}
as before.

7.2.
General AC Completion

Inference systems for completion modulo an equational theory which are
not restricted to the left-linear case usually need more inference
rules than the ones already covered in this article. For general AC
completion, however, there exists a particularly simple inference
system which constitutes a special case of normalized completion

[
Mar96
]
and can be found in Sarah Winkler’s PhD thesis

[
Win13
, p. 109]
.

{defi}

The inference system
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
is the same as
\mathsf{A}
for the fixed
theory AC but with a modified collapse rule which allows us to
rewrite with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
and the following rule which replaces
the two deduction rules of
\mathsf{A}
:

\textsf{deduce}\quad\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{E}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}}\quad\text{if $s\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}}$}}}$}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t$}

The purpose of this section is to show how
\mathsf{A}
can be simulated by

\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
in the case of
\mathcal{B}=\mathsf{AC}
. In addition to its theoretical
significance, this simulation result is also of practical importance
as it facilitates switching from
\mathsf{A}
to
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
in the middle of a
completion process instead of starting from scratch. We have more
to say about this at the end of this section.

Since local cliffs cannot be deduced
in
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
, the simulation has to work with a potentially smaller set of
rewrite rules. Furthermore, during a run, the variants of rules
stemming from local cliffs may be in different states with respect to
inter-reduction (
collapse
and
compose
). Given an intermediate TRS
\mathcal{R}
of a
run in
\mathsf{A}
as well as an intermediate TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of a run in

\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
, the invariant
\mathcal{R}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
resolves both
of the aforementioned problems. The main motivation behind this
invariant is the avoidance of
compose
and
collapse
in the
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
run.

Lemma 24
.

If
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
and

\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
then there exists a

TRS

\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\prime}
such that
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2})

and
\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
.

Proof 7.1
.

Let
>
be a fixed AC-compatible reduction order which is used in
both
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
. Suppose
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})

and
\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
. We perform a case
analysis on the inference rule applied in

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1})\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}_{2})
. The only interesting cases are
when
deduce
,
simplify
,
compose
or
collapse
are applied.

\triangleright

If
deduce
is applied, we further distinguish whether it was
applied to a local peak or cliff. In the case of a local cliff, we
have
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with

\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}r
. From
\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}r
and

\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
we obtain

\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}r
. Thus,

\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
holds. As

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})
is trivial, the
claim follows. In the case of a local peak, we have

\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\approx u\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with

t\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{1}}$}}}$}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}u
. Since

\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
holds, we have

t\,\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}\,v\,\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}}$}}}$}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\,s\,\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}}\,w\,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}u

for some
v
and
w
. By performing
deduce
and
simplify
steps

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptv\approx w\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\approx u\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,=\,(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})

is obtained. As
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
, the inclusion

\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
is trivial. Hence, the
claim holds.

\triangleright

If
simplify
is applied, we have
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
,

\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{0}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{0}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts^{\prime}\approx t^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}^{=}s^{\prime}

and
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}^{=}t^{\prime}
. By

\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
we have

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}s^{\prime}
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}t^{\prime}
.
Therefore, performing
simplify
, we obtain

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})
. As

\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}
, the inclusion

\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
is trivial.

\triangleright

If
compose
is applied, we have
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{2}
,

\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell^{\vphantom{1}}\to r\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with
r\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{0}/\mathsf{AC}}r^{\prime}
.
We have
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})
. Since
the inclusions

\mathcal{R}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
yield

\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}r\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}r^{\prime}
, we obtain

\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
.

\triangleright

If
collapse
is applied, we have

\mathcal{E}_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell^{\prime}\approx r\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}_{2}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell\to r\hskip 0.85358pt\}
with

\ell\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\ell^{\prime}
. By

\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
we have

\ell^{\prime}\,\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}\,t\,\mathrel{\prescript{}{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}{\leftarrow}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\,\ell\,\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\,u\,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\,r

for some
t
and
u
. Performing
deduce
and
simplify
, we obtain:

(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\approx u\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pt\ell^{\prime}\approx r\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})\,=\,(\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1})

By
\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
the
claim is concluded.

Theorem 25
.

For every fair run
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\vdash_{\mathsf{A}}^{*}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R})
there
exists a run
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
such
that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}
is an
AC
-complete presentation of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof 7.2
.

With a straightforward induction argument, we obtain the run

(\mathcal{E},\varnothing)\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\varnothing,\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
as well as

{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}^{+}}
(
\ast
) from
Lemma
24
.
Furthermore, AC termination of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and

{\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}}={\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}}
(
\ast\ast
)
are easy consequences of the definition of
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
. AC-completeness
of
\mathcal{R}
follows from fairness of the run in
\mathsf{A}
and
Theorem
11
. For the Church–Rosser modulo AC property of

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}
, consider a conversion
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}t
.
From (
\ast\ast
) we obtain
s\leftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}\hskip 0.85358pt\cup\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}
and
therefore
s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}}{\leftarrow}}t
by
the fact that
\mathcal{R}
is an AC-complete presentation of

\mathcal{E}
. Finally, (
\ast
) yields

s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}\hphantom{/}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}t
as
desired. Thus,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathsf{AC}
is an AC-complete presentation of
\mathcal{E}
.

In addition to the result of the previous theorem, the proof of
Lemma
24
provides a procedure to construct a
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
run which
“corresponds” to a given
\mathsf{A}
run. In particular, this means that
it is possible to switch from
\mathsf{A}
to
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
at any point while
performing AC completion. This is of practical relevance: Assume that
AC completion is started with
\mathsf{A}
in order to avoid AC
unification. If
\mathsf{A}
gets stuck due to simplified equations which
are not orientable into a left-linear rule or it seems to be the case
that the procedure diverges due to the problem described in
Example
7.1
, starting from scratch with
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
is not
necessary. We conclude the section by illustrating the practical
relevance of the simulation result with an example.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}
for abelian groups consisting of the equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\cdot x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x

\displaystyle x^{-}\cdot x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{e}

where
\cdot
is an AC symbol. Note that the well-known completion
run for non-abelian group theory is also a run in
\mathsf{A}
: Critical
pairs with respect to the associativity axiom are deducible via
local cliffs, non-left-linear intermediate rules are allowed and all
(intermediate) rules are orientable with e.g. AC-KBO. Hence, we obtain
the TRS
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
consisting of the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\cdot x

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 1$}\,}\,x

\displaystyle x^{-}\cdot(x\cdot y)

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 4$}\,}\,y

\displaystyle x\cdot x^{-}

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 7$}\,}\,\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle x^{-}\cdot x

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 2$}\,}}}\,\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle(x\cdot y)^{-}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 5$}\,}}}\,y^{-}\cdot x^{-}

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}^{-}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 8$}\,}}}\,\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle x^{--}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 3$}\,}}}\,x

\displaystyle x\cdot\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 6$}\,}}}\,x

\displaystyle x\cdot(x^{-}\cdot y)

\displaystyle\,\mathrel{\smash{\xrightarrow{\,\makebox[5.69054pt][c]{$\scriptstyle 9$}\,}}}\,y

and switch to
\mathsf{KB}_{\mathsf{AC}}
where we can collapse the redundant rules

4
,
6
,
7
and
9
. A final joinability check of all AC critical
pairs reveals that the resulting TRS
\mathcal{R}
is an AC-complete presentation
of abelian groups. Hence, the simulation result allows us to make progress
with
\mathsf{A}
even when it is doomed to fail. In particular, critical
pairs between rules whose left-hand sides do not contain AC symbols do
not need to be recomputed.

8.
Implementation

The command-line tool
accompll
implements AC completion for
left-linear TRSs based on the inference system
\mathsf{A}
(Definition
4.1
). It
uses external termination tools instead of a fixed AC-compatible
reduction order and is written in the programming language Haskell.
The source code of the tool
accompll
is available on
GitHub
2
2

2

https://github.com/niedjoh/accompll
. As input,
the tool expects a file in the
WST
3
3

3

https://www.lri.fr/~marche/tpdb/format.html
format
describing the equational theory on which left-linear AC completion
should be performed. The user can choose whether
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
,

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}
or
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
is used for rewriting in the inference
rules
simplify
and
compose
. Furthermore, the generation of critical pairs can
be restricted to the primality criterion. Note that most of
these options are facilitated by the theoretical results in the previous
sections. A discussion of
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}
will follow in Section
8.3
.

Another feature is the validity problem solving mode which solves a
given instance of the validity problem for an equational theory
\mathcal{E}

upon successful completion of
\mathcal{E}
. This mode can be triggered by
supplying a concrete equation
s\approx t
as a command line argument
in addition to the file describing
\mathcal{E}
.

In the tool
accompll
, external termination tools do much of the heavy
lifting. In particular, the user can supply the executable of an
arbitrary termination tool as long as the output starts with

YES
,
MAYBE
,
NO
or
TIMEOUT
(all
other cases are treated as an error). The input format for the
termination tool can be set by a command line argument. The available
options are the WST format as well as the XML format of the Nagoya
Termination Tool
[
YKS14
]
.
4
4

4

https://www.trs.cm.is.nagoya-u.ac.jp/NaTT/natt-xml.html

Since starting a new process for every call of the termination tool
causes a lot of operating system overhead, the tool supports an
interactive mode which allows it to communicate with a single process
of the termination tool in a dialogue style. Here, the only constraint
for the termination tool is that it accepts a sequence of termination
problems separated by the keyword
(RUN)
. This is currently
only implemented in an experimental version of Tyrolean Termination
Tool 2 (
T
T
T
2
)
[
KSZM09
]
, but we hope that more termination tools
will follow as this approach has a positive effect on the runtime of
completion with termination tools while demanding comparatively little
implementation effort. The remainder of this section discusses
important aspects and properties of the implementation.

8.1.
Termination Tools

The reduction order is a critical input parameter of a completion
procedure. Finding an appropriate order can be very challenging as the
equations which are generated during a completion run are usually not
known in advance. Hence, the nature of this problem is different from
the standard termination problem where the input is one specific TRS.

While it is well-known that termination is an undecidable property of
TRSs, a number of termination tools have been developed which can
solve the termination problem automatically in many practical cases.
The usage of termination tools in completion has been pioneered by
Wehrman et al.
[
WSW06
]
. Intuitively, the difficulty of finding an
appropriate reduction order is replaced by a sequence of calls to a
termination tool. This allows us to define a completion procedure
which does not depend on a reduction order as input. An important
ingredient for completion with termination tools is a

constraint system
which keeps track of all rules which have been
produced during the run. Checking termination for the constraint system as
opposed to the current TRS guarantees that there exists a single
reduction order which can be used for the whole run. This is important
as correctness is lost when the reduction order is changed during a
completion run
[
SK94
]
. The next definition extends the inference
system
\mathsf{A}
by a constraint system.

{defi}

The inference system
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
transforms triples consisting of an ES

\mathcal{E}
and TRSs
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{C}
over the common signature
\mathcal{F}
. Except
for
orient
, the inference rules are trivial extensions of the rules in

\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
where the constraint system
\mathcal{C}
is not changed.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{}}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{C}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}}&\text{if $\mathcal{C}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358pts\rightarrow t\hskip 0.85358pt\}$ is $\mathcal{B}$-terminating}\\
\textsf{orient}&&\\
&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}\uplus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\rightarrow s\hskip 0.85358pt\},\mathcal{C}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\rightarrow s\hskip 0.85358pt\}}&\text{if $\mathcal{C}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptt\rightarrow s\hskip 0.85358pt\}$ is $\mathcal{B}$-terminating}\end{array}

Note that
\mathcal{R}
is subject to insertion
as well as removal of rules while the constraint
system
\mathcal{C}
just acts as an accumulator of all orientations for the
termination check.
As usual,
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
denotes a
step in the inference system
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
and a sequence of steps starting
with
(\mathcal{E},\varnothing,\varnothing)
constitutes a run for
\mathcal{E}
. We
will now prove that
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
is sound and complete with respect to
\mathsf{A}
.

Lemma 26
.

For every run

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\cdots\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n}

there exists an equivalent run

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\cdots\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}

which uses the reduction order
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}
.

Proof 8.1
.

First of all, note that
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}
is a
\mathcal{B}
-compatible
reduction order as it is transitive, closed under contexts and
substitutions and the
\mathcal{B}
-termination of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is established by
induction on the length of the run in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
. Since the constraint
system is only altered and used in the
orient
rule, all other steps
are valid steps in
\mathsf{A}
by just removing the constraint system.
Since
\mathcal{C}_{n}
includes all rules which are oriented during the run,
the corresponding
orient
steps in
\mathsf{A}
also succeed.

Lemma 27
.

For every run

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\cdots\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}

using the
\mathcal{B}
-compatible reduction order
>
there exists an equivalent
run

\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\cdots\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}\,\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n}

where
{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}\subseteq{>}
.

Proof 8.2
.

We perform induction on
n
. For
n=0
we just translate

(\mathcal{E}_{0},\varnothing)
to
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\varnothing,\varnothing)
. For

n>0
we obtain
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{n-1},\mathcal{R}_{n-1},\mathcal{C}_{n-1})
and

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n-1}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}\subseteq{>}
from the induction
hypothesis. If the step

(\mathcal{E}_{n-1},\mathcal{R}_{n-1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}$ }}}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n})
is not an
application of
orient
we have

(\mathcal{E}_{n-1},\mathcal{R}_{n-1},\mathcal{C}_{n-1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})
where
\mathcal{C}_{n-1}=\mathcal{C}_{n}
and therefore

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}\subseteq{>}
as the constraint system is
just carried along without any further restrictions in all rules
except
orient
. Now assume that the step is an application of
orient
on
the equation
s\approx t
. We have

\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\setminus\{\hskip 0.85358pts\approx t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
and

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\mathcal{R}_{n-1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptv\rightarrow w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
where
v>w
and

\{\hskip 0.85358ptv,w\hskip 0.85358pt\}=\{\hskip 0.85358pts,t\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathcal{C}_{n-1}\cup\{\hskip 0.85358ptv\rightarrow w\hskip 0.85358pt\}
. Thus,

(\mathcal{E}_{n-1},\mathcal{R}_{n-1},\mathcal{C}_{n-1})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.84526pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})
by an application of
orient
and we obtain

(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})
as
well as
{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{C}_{n}/\mathcal{B}}^{+}}\subseteq{>}
from the induction
hypothesis together with
v>w
and the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
.

Note that the presented translation between runs of
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
allows
us to speak about the fairness of runs in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
since this simply
means that the corresponding run in
\mathsf{A}
is fair.

Lemma
26
shows that the usage of termination tools is a
sound extension which allows us to perform completion without
constructing an appropriate reduction order beforehand. Furthermore,
the usage of termination tools does not affect the applicability of
our completion procedure due to the previous completeness result
(Lemma
27
). However, implementing completion with
termination tools such that it does not affect the applicability in
practice is a highly nontrivial task. The reason for this is that
without a concrete reduction order, both versions of the
orient
rule may
be applicable. This yields a potentially huge search space as
orienting a rule in the “wrong” direction may cause completion to
fail or diverge. In
[
WSW06
]
this problem is solved by traversing
the search space which is a binary tree with some best-first strategy
based on a cost function which takes the number of equations, rules
and critical pairs into account.

The state of the art for solving this problem efficiently is

multi-completion with termination tools
due to
Winkler et al.
[
WSMK13
]
. This method traverses the whole search
space while being as efficient as possible by sharing computations between
different nodes in the binary tree which represents the search
space. The method is implemented in the tool
mkbTT
. As the
implementation of this approach is a major effort, we adopt the
strategy used by the automatic mode of the Knuth–Bendix Completion
Visualizer (
KBCV
)
[
SZ12
]
. Instead of traversing the whole search
space,
KBCV
runs two threads in parallel where one thread prefers to
orient equations from left to right while the other one prefers to
orient from right to left. If one of the threads finishes
successfully, the corresponding result is reported. Completion fails
if both threads fail. Needless to say, this compromises completeness,
but it is a trade-off which works well in many practical cases. In
particular,
KBCV
can also complete systems which
mkbTT
cannot within
a given time constraint
[
SZ12
]
.

8.2.
Strategy

The presentation of
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
as an inference system allows
implementations to use the given rules in any order as long as the
produced run is fair. This section is about the strategy for applying
rules of
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
which is employed by the tool
accompll
. From now on,
we specialize to the equational theory AC as
accompll
was developed
for this specific case.

The used strategy is based on Huet’s completion procedure

[
BN98
, Section 7.4]
. An important property of this procedure is
that the rules
simplify
,
delete
,
compose
and
collapse
are applied eagerly in order
to keep the intermediate ESs and TRSs as small as possible. Usually,
this has a positive effect on the runtime of the completion
process. Unlike Huet’s completion procedure, in the employed strategy
the orientation of a rule is always directly followed by the
computation of all possible critical pairs which involve this
rule. Furthermore,
orient
is only applied once per iteration to the
smallest equation with respect to the term size. This modified version
of Huet’s completion procedure is implemented e.g. in
KBCV
and the
flow chart depicted in Figure
1
is based on

[
SZ12
]
. However, in contrast to standard completion, we have to
add some of the critical pairs as rules in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}
. Hence, we apply

deduce
on the selected equation before
compose
and
collapse
are applied
exhaustively. Moreover, we need to keep track of a separate set of
pending rules (
\mathcal{P}
) as the eager recursive computation of critical
pairs between rules and AC axioms might unnecessarily lead to
non-termination of the completion procedure.

simplify
simplify

delete
collapse
\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{P}=\varnothing
success
orient
simplify

deduce
compose
choose
from
\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{P}
yes
to NF
new CPs
to NF

Figure 1.
Flow chart for
accompll
’s completion procedure.

We will now describe the flow chart depicted in Figure
1
in
detail. As already mentioned,
accompll
also keeps track of a list of
pending rules
\mathcal{P}
. Intuitively, a pending rule is like a normal
rule with the exception that critical pairs involving it have not yet
been computed and it is not used to collapse other rules. Hence,

accompll
works on a quadruple of an ES and three TRSs

(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{C})
which are processed by one main loop. First of
all,
simplify
is applied exhaustively such that both sides of every
equation are normal forms with respect to
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{P}
. This
preliminary step may be already enough to join some equations, so
after that every AC equivalent equation is removed with
delete
. If no
equations or pending rules are left, we are done. Otherwise, an
equation
\ell\approx r
or pending rule
\ell\rightarrow r
which is minimal
with respect to
|\ell|+|r|
where
|\cdot|
denotes the size of a
term is selected. If it is a rule,
orient
is just the identity
function. Otherwise,
orient
is applied with the orientation preference
of the given thread, i.e., it first orients the equation in the
preferred direction and only tries the other option in case of
failure. An important feature of the implementation of
orient
is that it
can be postponed, i.e., if one equation is not orientable in either
direction, the next one is tried. If
orient
never succeeds, the thread
terminates in a failure state.

In any case, a successful application of
orient
yields some rule

\rho
. Next,
deduce
is used to produce the sets
\mathsf{CP}({\{\hskip 0.85358pt\rho\hskip 0.85358pt\}})
and

\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathcal{R},\{\hskip 0.85358pt\rho\hskip 0.85358pt\}})
which are added to
\mathcal{E}
as well as

\mathsf{CP}^{\pm}({\mathsf{AC}^{\pm},\{\hskip 0.85358pt\rho\hskip 0.85358pt\}})
which is added to
\mathcal{P}
.
The computation of critical pairs is of course restricted to the
primality criterion if the corresponding option is set.

After that, all
rules in
\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{R}
are exhaustively composed such that their
right-hand sides are normal forms with respect to
\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{R}
.
Finally,
collapse
is applied exhaustively in
\mathcal{R}
with respect to
\rho

and in
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
\{\hskip 0.85358pt\rho\hskip 0.85358pt\}\cup\mathcal{R}
. Here, we
distinguish between
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{P}
because at this point, the
left-hand sides of
\mathcal{R}
are known to be normal forms of the remaining
rules of
\mathcal{R}
while this can not be assured for the deduced rules

\mathcal{P}
. Note that we do not collapse
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}
as it
would require checking rules for equality.

In order to produce a left-linear system,
orient
is only applied if the
left-hand side of the resulting rule is linear which makes all
intermediate TRSs left-linear. Fairness only demands left-linearity
for the resulting TRS, but improving this aspect was not considered as
it is unclear based on which criteria non-left-linear rules should be
turned back into equations again. Moreover, keeping each intermediate
TRS left-linear makes intermediate rules stemming from critical pairs
left-linear by definition: Since the AC axioms are linear, overlaps
between the AC axioms and a left-linear TRS always produce linear
terms. Thus, the left-linearity of pending rules does not have to be
checked as rewriting with linear rules preserves linearity. We are now
ready to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 28
.

The
TRS
s produced by the tool
accompll
are
AC
-canonical
presentations of the
ES
provided as input.

Proof 8.3
.

Suppose that given an ES
\mathcal{E}
,
accompll
outputs a TRS
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and
consider the thread which produced this result in
n
iterations of
the main loop as depicted in the flow chart (Figure
1
).
In particular, let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
\mathcal{R}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{i}
denote the
respective values of
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{C}
at the decision
node in the flow chart in the
i
-th iteration. Note that

\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\mathcal{R}_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{0}=\varnothing
and

\mathcal{E}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{n}=\varnothing
. It is immediate from the flow chart
and its textual description that

(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}\,\cdots\,\mathrel{\hbox{\vrule\vphantom{$\textstyle\vdash$}}\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\vbox{\halign{\hfil\kern 1pt$\tsstyle#\vphantom{by}$\kern 1pt\hfil\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr\kern 1.0pt\hrule\kern 1.0pt\cr.0pt$\scriptstyle\hphantom{\vdash}\kern-2.0pt\vphantom{by}$\kern 1.0\cr}}$}}_{\smash{\raisebox{2.27621pt}{\scriptsize$\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{TT}}$ }}}^{\smash{\raisebox{-4.55244pt}{$\scriptstyle*$}}}\,(\mathcal{E}_{n},\mathcal{R}_{n}\cup\mathcal{P}_{n},\mathcal{C}_{n})

is a run for
\mathcal{E}
. Furthermore, all prime critical pairs of
\mathcal{R}_{n}

have been considered as an intermediate equation and all prime
critical pairs between
\mathcal{R}_{n}
and
\mathsf{AC}^{\pm}
have been considered
as an intermediate rule. The fact that
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is an AC-complete
presentation of
\mathcal{E}
now follows from fairness (Definition
4.1
)
together with Lemma
26
and Theorem
11
.
Finally, a straightforward induction argument shows that the
statements

(1)

\ell\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{R}_{i}})
for every
{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\mathcal{P}_{i}
and

(2)

r\in\mathsf{NF}({\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\mathcal{P}_{i}})
for every
{\ell\rightarrow r}\in\mathcal{R}_{i}\cup\mathcal{P}_{i}

hold for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Together, (1) and (2) show that
\mathcal{R}_{n}

is also AC-canonical: Just like in the proof of Lemma
20

we conclude that if each rule of an AC-complete system is
right-reduced, it is also right-AC-reduced.

8.3.
Implementation Details

The implementation of the tool
accompll
is based on the Haskell

term-rewriting
library
[
FAS13
]
which takes care of most
of our needs regarding term rewriting except rewriting modulo AC,
prime critical pairs and the computation of normal forms. The
following paragraphs describe selected implementation details.

Rewriting to normal forms

A naive implementation of the
computation of normal forms is highly inefficient as in general, the
whole term has to be traversed for every rewrite step. As a trade-off
between the time-consuming implementation of sophisticated term
indexing techniques and the naive implementation we employ a bottom-up
construction of normal forms by innermost rewriting of marked terms
where normal form positions are remembered.

AC equivalence of terms

Checking whether two terms are
equivalent modulo AC is needed for the implementation of the inference
rule
deduce
. To this end, an equation
s\approx t
is transformed into
a canonical form
s^{\prime}\approx t^{\prime}
where nested applications of AC
symbols are flattened out to just one
n
-ary application for
arbitrary
n
and their arguments are ordered with respect some total
order on terms. Then,
s\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}t
if and only if
s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
. As an
example, the terms
\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{f}(y,\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(z,\mathsf{a}))))
and

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(y,x),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},z)))
with
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{AC}

are AC equivalent because they have the same canonical form

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},z)),x,y)
with respect to
the lexicographic order on the representation of terms as strings of
ASCII symbols.

Normal forms in rewriting modulo AC

As already mentioned in Section
2
, a direct implementation of

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
cannot be efficient. In the following, we will prove that it
suffices to implement the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}
from Peterson and
Stickel
[
PS81
]
which is easier to compute but relies on AC
matching. Although there are more efficient implementations, we used
the AC matching algorithm due to Contejean
[
Con04
]
as it has been
certified. The result we are about to prove
() is a special case of more general
results for conditional rewriting modulo an equational theory by
Meseguer
[
Mes17
, Corollary 3]
. However, to the best of our
knowledge, the literature does not contain a direct proof of the
required result despite its importance for effective implementations
also in the unconditional case. Therefore, in the following, we give a
detailed and direct proof of the result.

We start with recalling the definition of the concept of
extended rules due to
[
PS81
]
.
Let
\mathcal{R}
be a TRS and let
f(u,v)\rightarrow r
be a rule in
\mathcal{R}
where

f
is an AC function symbol. The rule
f(f(u,v),x)\rightarrow f(r,x)
with

x\in{\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({f(u,v)})}
is an
extension
of

f(u,v)\rightarrow r
. The TRS consisting of
\mathcal{R}
together with all
extensions of rules in
\mathcal{R}
is denoted by
\mathcal{R}^{e}
. We will now
prove that the relations
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}
and

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}
coincide. To this end, we show

{{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}
,
which is called
strict coherence
in
[
Mes17
]
.

Lemma 29
.

{\mathrel{\prescript{}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{=}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}}

Proof 8.4
.

Suppose
t\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}s\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{q}u
. We show

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{=}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}u
by induction on
s
. Let
\ell_{1}\to r_{1}

and
\ell_{2}\to r_{2}
be the rules employed in the left and right
steps.
We distinguish four cases.
We use the facts that

\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{\epsilon}
and

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{\epsilon}
coincide
and

\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathsf{AC}}$}}}$}}
is contained in
\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}
.

(1)

If
q=\epsilon
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}u
. Thus, the claim holds.

(2)

If
p=\epsilon
and
q\notin\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({\ell_{1}})
then
s=\ell_{1}\sigma

and

t=r_{1}\sigma
for some substitution
\sigma
and there exists a variable
position
p^{\prime}
in
\ell_{1}
with
p^{\prime}\leqslant q
.
We have
\ell_{1}\sigma|_{p^{\prime}}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}u|_{p^{\prime}}
.
Define the substitution
\tau
as follows:

\tau(x)\,=\,\begin{cases}u|_{p^{\prime}}&\text{if $x=\ell_{1}|_{p^{\prime}}$}\\
\sigma(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

As
r_{1}
is linear and
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({\ell_{1}})=\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({r_{1}})
,
the variable
\ell_{1}|_{p^{\prime}}
has one occurrence in
r_{1}
.
Hence, we obtain

t=r_{1}\sigma\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\mathsf{AC}}r_{1}\tau\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathsf{AC}}$}}}$}}\ell_{1}\tau=u
.

(3)

If
p=ip^{\prime}
and
q=jq^{\prime}
with
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
then we further
distinguish two subcases. If
i=j
then

t|_{i}\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathsf{AC}}$}}}$}}s|_{i}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}u|_{i}
and we obtain

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{=}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}u
with help of the induction hypothesis.
If
i\neq j
then we clearly have

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{q}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{p}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}u
.

(4)

In the final case,
p=\epsilon
and
q\in\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({\ell_{1}})
with

q\neq\epsilon
. Since
\mathcal{P}\mathsf{os}_{\mathcal{F}}({\ell_{1}})\subseteq\{\hskip 0.85358pt\epsilon,1\hskip 0.85358pt\}
, the
rule
\ell_{1}\to r_{1}
is an associativity rule

f(f(x,y),z)\rightarrow f(x,f(y,z))
and
q=1
. So
s|_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\ell_{2}\sigma

and
u=f(r_{2}\sigma,s|_{2})
. If
\ell_{2}\to r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
then the
corresponding extended rule results in

f(\ell_{2}\sigma,s|_{2})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e}}^{\epsilon}f(r_{2}\sigma,s|_{2})

and thus

t\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}s\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}f(\ell_{2}\sigma,s|_{2})\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e}}^{\epsilon}u
,
which entails
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}u
. Otherwise,
\ell_{2}\to r_{2}
is the extension

f(\ell,x^{\prime})\to f(r,x^{\prime})
of some rule
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
with

x^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{V}\mathsf{ar}({\ell})
. Define the substitution
\tau
as follows:

\tau(x)\,=\,\begin{cases}f(x^{\prime}\sigma,s|_{2})&\text{if $x=x^{\prime}$}\\
\sigma(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Since
t\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}s\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}f(\ell_{2}\sigma,s|_{2})\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}f(\ell\sigma,f(x^{\prime}\sigma,s|_{2}))=\ell_{2}\tau
, we obtain

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{\epsilon}r_{2}\tau
. Moreover,

r_{2}\tau=f(r\sigma,f(x^{\prime}\sigma,s|_{2}))\mathrel{\reflectbox{${\rightarrow_{\reflectbox{${\scriptstyle\mathsf{AC}}$}}}$}}f(f(r\sigma,x^{\prime}\sigma),s|_{2}))=u
.

Lemma 30
.

{{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

Proof 8.5
.

Lemma
29
generalizes to

{{\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}}}\subseteq{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}}

by a straightforward induction argument. From

\mathsf{AC}\subseteq{\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}}
we obtain

{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathsf{AC}}{\leftarrow}}}
and hence the claim follows.

Theorem 31
.

The relations
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}
coincide.

Proof 8.6
.

Using Lemma
30
we obtain

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}\,=\,{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}\,\subseteq\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

The other direction follows from the definition of
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}
as well as the
fact that
{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}={\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e}}}
:

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}}\,\subseteq\,{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e}}}\,=\,{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}}\,\subseteq\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}

Corollary 32
.

The relations
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{*}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}

coincide.

Proof 8.7
.

It is sufficient to show

{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}^{n}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}={\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{n}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

for all
n\geqslant 0
. We use induction on
n
. If
n=0
then the
claim is trivial. If
n>0
then the claim is verified as follows:

\displaystyle{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}^{n}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

\displaystyle\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}^{n-1}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}

(
{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}\,=\,{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}
)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{n-1}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}

(induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{n-1}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}

(
{\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}}
)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{n-1}\cdot\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

(Theorem
31
)

\displaystyle\,=\,{\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{e},\hskip 0.85358pt\mathsf{AC}}^{n}\cdot\sim_{\mathsf{AC}}}

9.
Experimental Results

We evaluated the performance of our tool
accompll
on a problem
set containing 52 ESs. The problem set is based on the one used in

[
WM11
]
and has been extended by further examples from the
literature as well as handcrafted examples. From the 52 ESs, 10
contain equations which cannot be oriented into a left-linear
rule. Furthermore, 6 problems are ground which means that
accompll
cannot find a finite solution by Example
7.1
. This leaves
us with 36 problems for which
accompll
may find an AC complete
presentation. However, for some of these ESs, simplified equations
where both terms are non-linear may be deduced which causes

accompll
to get stuck. Furthermore, many interesting problems
exhibit the properties described in Examples
7.1

and
7.1
and are therefore out of reach for our
method. Nevertheless, there are 7 examples in the problem set where
left-linear completion with AC axioms is preferable to general AC
completion due to significantly better performance.

The experiments were performed on an Intel Core
i7-7500U running at a clock rate of 2.7 GHz with 15.5 GiB of main
memory. Our tool
accompll
was used with the termination tool
T
T
T
2
as
well as an experimental version (denoted by
T
T
T
2
e
) which allows our
tool to communicate a sequence of termination problems without having
to start a new process all the time, as described in the preceding
section.

Table 1.
Experimental results on 52 problems (excerpt)

accompll

Table
1
compares the two configurations of
accompll
with the
normalized completion
[
Mar96
]
mode of
mkbTT

[
WM13
]
and the AC
completion mode of
MædMax

[
Win19
]
on selected examples. The tool

mkbTT
is the original implementation of multi-completion with
termination tools
[
WSMK13
]
.
MædMax
, on the other hand,
implements
maximal completion

[
KH11
]
which makes use of
MaxSAT/MaxSMT solvers instead of termination tools in order to avoid
using concrete reduction orders as input. To the best of our
knowledge, there is no other comparable completion tool which
supports AC axioms. Since normalized completion subsumes general AC
completion, a comparison with the aforementioned modes of both systems
allows us to assess the effectiveness of
accompll
with respect to the
state of the art in AC completion. Note that both normalized completion
and general AC completion use AC unification.

In Table
1
, columns (1) show the execution time in seconds
where
\infty
denotes that the timeout of 60 seconds has been reached
and
\bot
denotes failure of completion. Columns (2) state the number
of rules of the completed TRS. In Example
4.1
, the
equations just have to be oriented and one additional rule has to be
added in the case of left-linear AC completion. Hence, all three
systems can handle this problem easily. Examples
7.1

and
7.2
show the two main limitations of left-linear AC
completion: it diverges on problems which contain an AC symbol where
both arguments have “structure” and non-left-linear presentations
are out of reach. For general AC completion and normalized
completion, respectively, these examples pose no problem. The
remaining examples show that the absence of AC unification can make
left-linear completion more practical than general AC completion.

{exa}

The Eckmann–Hilton argument
[
EH62
]
considers the
following equational theory
\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}\oplus a

\displaystyle\,\approx\,a

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\otimes a

\displaystyle\,\approx\,a

\displaystyle(a\oplus b)\otimes(c\oplus d)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(a\otimes c)\oplus(b\otimes d)

\displaystyle a\oplus\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,a

\displaystyle a\otimes\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,a

and proves
\mathsf{0}\approx\mathsf{1}
.
5
5

5

This example was
brought to our attention by Vincent van Oostrom.
Moreover, it
establishes that
\oplus
and
\otimes
coincide and are
associative as well as commutative. If we assume that
\oplus
and

\otimes
are AC symbols,
accompll
produces the following complete
presentation
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathcal{E}
in less than a second:

\displaystyle b\oplus a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\otimes a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle a\otimes\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

In Table
1
, we can see that

mkbTT
needs considerably more time to complete
\mathcal{E}
and
MædMax
even times out after 60 seconds. By inspecting
\mathcal{R}
, it is
easily seen that the operations as well as the unit
elements are equivalent. Furthermore, the concise presentation of

\mathcal{E}
as a complete TRS is facilitated by our canonicity results as
well as the implementation of inter-reduction (
collapse
and
compose
). Using
the inference system
\mathsf{B}
without inter-reduction and with the same AC
compatible reduction order, we obtain a much larger complete
but non-canonical presentation of
\mathcal{E}
which extends
\mathcal{R}
:

\displaystyle b\oplus a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}\oplus a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

\displaystyle(a\oplus b)\otimes(c\oplus d)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(a\otimes c)\oplus(b\otimes d)

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle a\oplus\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

\displaystyle a\otimes(b\oplus c)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(\mathsf{0}\otimes b)\oplus(a\otimes c)

\displaystyle\mathsf{1}\otimes a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}\otimes a

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

\displaystyle(\mathsf{0}\otimes a)\oplus b

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a\oplus b

\displaystyle a\otimes\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,a

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{append}(\mathsf{nil},l)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,l

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{s}(y)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{s}(x+y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{append}(\mathsf{c}(x,l_{1}),l_{2})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{c}(x,\mathsf{append}(l_{1},l_{2}))

\displaystyle\mathsf{add}(\mathsf{nil},\mathsf{nil})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{rev}(\mathsf{nil})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{nil}

\displaystyle\mathsf{add}(\mathsf{c}(x,l),\mathsf{nil})

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x+\mathsf{add}(l,\mathsf{nil})

\displaystyle\mathsf{rev}(\mathsf{c}(x,l))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{append}(\mathsf{rev}(l),\mathsf{c}(x,\mathsf{nil}))

\displaystyle\mathsf{add}(\mathsf{nil},\mathsf{c}(x,l))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x+\mathsf{add}(\mathsf{nil},l)

\displaystyle\mathsf{rev}(\mathsf{rev}(l))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,l

\displaystyle\mathsf{add}(\mathsf{c}(x,l_{1}),\mathsf{c}(y,l_{2}))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x+y)+\mathsf{add}(l_{1},l_{2})

together with the AC axioms for
+
. This is an extension of

[
Ave95
, Exercise 4.2.4(b)]
(the addition operation on lists) with
the standard append and reverse functions on lists.
We added the involution axiom for list reversal
in order to
generate critical pairs. Our tool
accompll
produces a
complete presentation in less than a second by orienting all
equations in
\mathcal{E}
from left to right and adding the following rules:

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+x

\displaystyle\rightarrow x

\displaystyle\mathsf{rev}(\mathsf{append}(l,\mathsf{c}(x,\mathsf{nil})))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{c}(x,\mathsf{rev}(l))

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)+y

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathsf{s}(y+x)

It takes
mkbTT
more than 60 seconds to solve this problem and

MædMax
terminates with an error message.

{exa}

Consider the ES
\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}+x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}\times x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)+y

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{s}(x+y)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)\times y

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x\times y)+y

\displaystyle\mathsf{0}-x

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle(x+y)\times z

\displaystyle\,\approx\,(x\times z)+(y\times z)

\displaystyle x-\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x

\displaystyle\mathsf{div}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{s}(y))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(x)-\mathsf{s}(y)

\displaystyle\,\approx\,x-y

\displaystyle\mathsf{div}(\mathsf{s}(x),\mathsf{s}(y))

\displaystyle\,\approx\,\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{div}(x-y,\mathsf{s}(y)))

together with the AC axioms for
+
and
\times
, defining
addition, multiplication, cutoff subtraction and round-up division
on the natural numbers. Our tool
accompll
produced a complete
presentation in less than a second by orienting all equations in

\mathcal{E}
from left to right and adding the following rules:

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,x

\displaystyle x\times\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{0}

\displaystyle x+\mathsf{s}(y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,\mathsf{s}(y+x)

\displaystyle x\times\mathsf{s}(y)

\displaystyle\,\rightarrow\,(y\times x)+x

Since round-up division cannot be handled by simplification orders

[
Der79
]
, this example also shows the merits of using termination
tools in completion. Note that it takes
mkbTT
much longer to
complete
\mathcal{E}
(more than 9 seconds) and
MædMax
times out on this
problem after 60 seconds.

Despite the successes in solving the previous three examples
quickly, the severity of the limitations of left-linear AC
completion is reflected in the total number of solved problems as
shown in Table
1
. In particular, the problem set does not
contain an ES which is only completed by
accompll
. However, given
Theorem
25
, this is not unexpected. Another noteworthy but
unsurprising fact is that complete systems produced by
accompll
tend
to have more rules since every rule needs different versions of
left-hand sides to facilitate rewriting without AC matching. The
complete results are available on the tool’s
website.
6
6

6

http://cl-informatik.uibk.ac.at/software/accompll/

In addition to full details for the experiments with
T
T
T
2
as
shown in Table
1
, the website also contains additional
experiments with the termination tool
MU-TERM

[
GL20
]
. For

accompll
, using
MU-TERM
instead of
T
T
T
2
does not change the set
of solved problems but it usually takes more time to complete ESs.
The situation is different for
mkbTT
: Using
MU-TERM
instead of

T
T
T
2
internally, it can complete fewer systems overall (26 instead
of 38 out of 52). However, five of these systems are not completed
by
mkbTT
with
T
T
T
2
and three of them are not completed by any
other tool configuration.
We conclude with some additional comments on the results.

\triangleright

The results are not cluttered with detailed results for the
available options regarding prime critical pairs and the concrete
rewrite relation used for
simplify
and
compose
since they did not lead to
significant runtime differences. Instead, the default options (no
prime critical pairs and the rewrite relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
) were used
for the experiments.

\triangleright

The restriction to prime critical pairs did not pay off in the
experimental results. However, it may reduce the number of critical
pairs which have to be considered even if inter-reduction is applied
eagerly (see Example
6.2
).
Therefore, apart from the theoretical relevance of its feasibility,
we consider it an important improvement over the status quo which could
be beneficial for users of
accompll
in the future.

\triangleright

The possibility to choose the concrete rewrite relation
used for
simplify
and
compose
also did not lead to significant improvements
in our experiments. However, simplifying with
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}/\mathsf{AC}}
can boost performance
since the problem can be simplified even before a rule with a fitting
left-hand side is derived.

\triangleright

Due to the incompleteness of the used approach for completion
with termination tools, some equations in the problems

A95_ex4_2_4a.trs
as well as
sp.trs
had to be
reversed in order to get appropriate results. Note that this does
not distort the experimental results for left-linear AC completion
in general as the problem lies in the particular implementation of
completion with termination tools.

10.
Conclusion

This article consolidates and extends existing work on
left-linear
\mathcal{B}
-completion
[
Hue80
,
Bac91
,
Ave95
]
by using and
adapting elegant proof techniques which have been put forward for
standard completion in
[
HMSW19
]
. This approach allowed for
improvements of existing results: Huet’s result could be
strengthened to prime critical pairs in Theorem
6
which in turn
improved the definition of fair runs in the inference system
\mathsf{A}
(Definition
4.1
). Furthermore, the usage of peak-and-cliff
decreasingness instead of proof orderings simplified the proof of
the correctness result for
\mathsf{A}
(Theorem
11
). The
limitation to finite runs also facilitated the removal of the
encompassment condition which allows the inference system to produce
smaller
\mathcal{B}
-complete systems as we showed in
Example
4.1
.

In addition, the relationship between the two existing inference
systems from the literature has been investigated thoroughly, its
core part being a simulation result (Theorem
14
) which states
that any fair run in
\mathsf{B}
can be simulated by a fair run in
\mathsf{A}
. With
the novel simulation result, completion with
\mathsf{B}
can be reduced to
completion with
\mathsf{A}
in the case of finite runs.
Furthermore, the presentation of novel canonicity results
facilitates a concrete definition of minimal complete systems in our
setting. Since the inference system
\mathsf{A}
adds critical pairs
stemming from overlaps between (intermediate) rules and equations in

\mathcal{B}
, its termination relies heavily on inter-reduction and
therefore canonicity (Example
6.2
).
The final theoretical contribution of this article is a formal
presentation of the correspondence between left-linear AC completion
and general AC completion through another simulation result
(Theorem
25
).

The tool
accompll
is the first implementation of left-linear AC
completion. Our novel results on canonicity define in which way the
systems produced by
accompll
are minimal and unique.
Unfortunately, the experimental results show that despite the
practical advantages of avoiding AC unification and deciding
validity problems with the normal rewrite relation, left-linear AC
completion often needs infinitely many rules and therefore
diverges. This problem does not seem to be mentioned in the
literature. However, the possible speedup due to the
avoidance of AC unification reported in the experimental results as
well as the already mentioned simulation result for general AC
completion show that left-linear AC completion also has merits in
practice.

We conclude by giving some pointers for future work. First of all, the
merits of our novel simulation result for general AC completion could
be evaluated experimentally by providing an implementation. While

accompll
adopts the two-thread version of
multi-completion
[
SZ12
]
, we anticipate that left-linear AC
completion can also be effectively implemented by a variant of
maximal completion that aims to find a canonical system
[
SW15
,
Hir21
]
.
Another interesting research
direction is normalized completion for the left-linear case. If
successful, this would facilitate the treatment of important cases
such as abelian groups despite the restriction to left-linear
TRSs. Furthermore, a formalization of the established theoretical
results is desirable. To that end, the existing Isabelle/HOL
formalization from
[
HMSW19
]
is a perfect starting point as some
results of this article are extensions of the results for standard
rewriting presented there.
